\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(6)

and proving the required coherence conditions. This is detailed in

[
CCRW17
]
. In fact the reasoning can
be simplified by the recent observation that it is sufficient to
define morphisms between strategies on
+
-covered configurations

[
Cla24
, Lemma 6.3.4]
so that we may define the horizontal
composition simply pointwise, with

(g\odot f)(x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma})=g(x^{\tau})\odot f(x^{\sigma})

for all
x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\sigma)
and
x^{\tau}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\tau)

causally compatible. All the necessary verifications for constructing a
bicategory follow rather easily
[
Cla24
, Theorem 6.4.11]
, and it is
only a minor extension to define the full double-categorical structure
(see also

[
Paq20
]
).

4.2.4.
Symmetric monoidal structure.

We show that
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}
is
a symmetric monoidal double category. The tensor product of games

A\otimes B
extends to strategies: if
\sigma:A\vdash A^{\prime}
and

\tau:B\vdash B^{\prime}
, then
\sigma\otimes\tau
is given by

\sigma\otimes\tau\overset{\partial_{\sigma}\otimes\partial_{\tau}}{\longrightarrow}(A\vdash B)\otimes(A^{\prime}\vdash B)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}(A\otimes A^{\prime})\vdash(B\otimes B^{\prime})

and similarly for 2-cells. All coherence data can be defined
using that the category of event structures and maps is symmetric
monoidal under
\otimes
; in particular the empty game gives the
monoidal unit. There is a monoidal interchange law given by the
invertible, globular 2-cell

(\tau\odot\sigma)\otimes(\tau^{\prime}\odot\sigma^{\prime})\to(\tau\otimes\tau^{\prime})\odot(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})
that sends
(x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma})\otimes(x^{\tau^{\prime}}\odot x^{\sigma^{\prime}})
to
(x^{\tau}\otimes x^{\tau^{\prime}})\odot(x^{\sigma}\otimes x^{\sigma^{\prime}})
.

Proposition 11
.

The double category
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}
has a symmetric monoidal structure given
by the tensor product
\otimes
of event structures, which extends to
games, strategies, maps of games, and 2-cells.

Proof 4.5

(Proof sketch)
.

The required structural vertical morphisms are maps of event
structures representing the symmetry, associativity, and unit
properties of the tensor product. All axioms are verified by elementary reasoning.

4.2.5.
Companions in
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}
.

Not all vertical morphisms in

\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}
admit a horizontal companion, but all vertical isomorphisms
do. The idea is as follows. For an isomorphism
h:A\to B
between games, the composite map

\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}\xrightarrow{\partial_{\mathbf{\hskip 1.1111ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}}}A\vdash A\xrightarrow{A\vdash h}A\vdash B

is a strategy because re-indexing along an isomorphism preserves the
strategy axioms. We call this strategy
\widehat{h}:A\vdash B
.

In addition, there is a pair of 2-cells of type

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

given by the diagrams below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\vdash A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\vdash B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\vdash B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\hskip 1.55554ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{\mathbf{\hskip 1.1111ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{\mathbf{\hskip 1.1111ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\vdash h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\vdash B}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\vdash A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\vdash A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\vdash B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{\mathbf{\hskip 1.1111ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{\mathbf{\hskip 1.1111ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\vdash h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\vdash h}

The two companion axioms are easy to verify.

Proposition 12
.

The double category
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}
has all companions of vertical
isomorphisms.

Applying Proposition
2
, we deduce:

Corollary 13
.

The bicategory
\mathbf{CG}
, equipped with the tensor product
\otimes
and
the associated structural data, is symmetric monoidal.

4.3.
A static collapse of concurrent games

We are ready to define our first collapse from dynamic to static
semantics. Formally, we describe an oplax double functor

\|-\|:\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbb{P}\mathbf{RRel}
, where
oplax
means that
composition is not preserved: instead we have globular 2-cells

\|\tau\odot\sigma\|\to\|\tau\|\circ\|\sigma\|
which
embed the causally compatible pairs into the matching pairs.

The image of a plain game
A
is the set
\mathscr{C}(A)
. To a
strategy
\sigma:A\vdash B
, we associate

\|\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{B})=\{x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\sigma)\mid\partial_{\sigma}(x^{\sigma})=x_{A}\vdash x_{B}\}

yielding a proof-relevant relation
\|\sigma\|
from
\mathscr{C}(A)
to

\mathscr{C}(B)
. To a 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

we associate the family of functions mapping
x^{\sigma}\in\|\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{B})
to
f(x^{\sigma})\in\|\sigma^{\prime}\|(h(x_{A}),k(x_{B}))
, for
x_{A}\in A
and
x_{B}\in B
.

Proposition
8
induces an isomorphism of

\|\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}\|
with the identity proof-relevant relation. From
Proposition
9
we obtain a function

\|\tau\odot\sigma\|\to\|\tau\|\circ\|\sigma\|
. This
is non-invertible in general, because some matching pairs are not
causally compatible.

Finally, there is a bijection

\|A\otimes B\|\cong\|A\|\otimes\|B\|
for games
A
and

B
, which extends to an isomorphism of proof-relevant relations

\|\sigma\otimes\tau\|\cong\|\sigma\|\otimes\|\tau\|
,
and a similar situation on 2-cells. Overall the double functor (strongly)
preserves the symmetric monoidal structure.

Theorem 14
.

This data determines a monoidal oplax double functor
\|-\|:\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbb{P}\mathbf{RRel}
.

We omit the proof, as the theorem is a special case of the more
elaborate version to come, which includes symmetries. Note that, although this
involves a lot of algebraic data, we emphasize that it would be
incomparably harder to establish a similar result directly at the
bicategorical level. (We discuss a lifting of this theorem to the respective
bicategories in §
6.2
, when required for our
applications in semantics.)

In summary, we can regard
\mathbf{CG}
as a dynamic version of
\mathbf{PRRel}
, where
the witnesses in
\mathbf{PRRel}
are reached over time and composition is
sensitive to deadlocks.

5.
Accommodating Symmetry

In this section, we look at a model of concurrent games enriched with
symmetry, known as thin concurrent games

[
CCW19
]
. We start by
explaining the basics of event structures with symmetry and
thin concurrent games (§
5.1
). Then we explain how
strategies in thin concurrent games can be viewed as distributors (§
5.2
).
We then define the double category
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
(§
5.3
) and
construct a monoidal oplax functor

\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}\to\mathbb{D}\mathbf{ist}
(§
5.4
). Finally we discuss the exponential modality (§
5.5
).

5.1.
Symmetry and thin concurrent games

Recall that we went from
\mathbf{PRRel}
to
\mathbf{Dist}
by replacing sets with
groupoids. We now go from
\mathbf{CG}
to
\mathbf{TCG}
by replacing the set of
configurations
\mathscr{C}(A)
with a groupoid of configurations

\mathscr{S}(A)
whose morphisms are chosen bijections called

symmetries
, that behave well with respect to the causal order.

5.1.1.
Event structures with symmetry

Our model is based on the following notion of event structure with
symmetry
[
Win07
]
:

{defi}
An
isomorphism family
on es
E
is a groupoid
\mathscr{S}(E)

having as objects all configurations, and as morphisms certain
bijections between configurations, satisfying:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{\emph{restriction:}}&\text{for all~$\theta:x\simeq y\in\mathscr{S}(E)$ and $x\supseteq x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}(E)$,}\\
&\text{there is $\theta\supseteq\theta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}(E)$
such that $\theta^{\prime}:x^{\prime}\simeq y^{\prime}$.}\\
\text{\emph{extension:}}&\text{for all $\theta:x\simeq y\in\mathscr{S}(E)$, $x\subseteq x^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}(E)$,}\\
&\text{there is $\theta\subseteq\theta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}(E)$ such that $\theta^{\prime}:x^{\prime}\simeq y^{\prime}$.}\end{array}

We call
(E,\mathscr{S}(E))
an
event structure with symmetry (
ess
)
.

We refer to morphisms in
\mathscr{S}(E)
as
symmetries
, and write

\theta:x\cong_{E}y
if
\theta:x\simeq y
with
\theta\in\mathscr{S}(E)
. The
domain

\mathrm{dom}(\theta)
of
\theta:x\cong_{E}y

is
x
, and likewise its
codomain

\mathrm{cod}(\theta)
is
y
.
A
map of ess

E\to F
is a map of event
structures that preserves symmetry: the bijection

f\theta\qquad\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\qquad fx\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f^{-1}}}{{\simeq}}\quad x\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta}}{{\simeq}}\quad y\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\simeq}}\quad fy,

is in
\mathscr{S}(F)
for
every
\theta:x\cong_{E}y
(recall that
f
restricted to any
y
is
bijective). This makes
f:\mathscr{S}(E)\to\mathscr{S}(F)
a functor of groupoids.

We can define a 2-category
\mathbf{ESS}
of ess, maps of ess, and natural
transformations between the induced functors. For
f,g:E\to F
such
a natural transformation is necessarily unique

[
Win07
]
, and corresponds to the fact that
for every
x\in\mathscr{C}(E)
the composite bijection

f\,x\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f^{-1}}}{{\simeq}}\quad x\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\simeq}}\quad g\,x

via local injectivity of
f
and
g
, is in
\mathscr{S}(F)
.
So this is an equivalence, denoted
f\sim g
.

5.1.2.
Thin games

We define games with symmetry. To
match the polarized structure, a game is an ess with two
sub-symmetries, one for each player (see
e.g.
[
Mel03
,
CCW19
,
Paq22
]
).

{defi}

A
thin concurrent game (tcg)
is a game
A
with
isomorphism families
\mathscr{S}(A),\mathscr{S}_{+}(A),\mathscr{S}_{-}(A)
s.t.
\mathscr{S}_{+}(A),\mathscr{S}_{-}(A)\subseteq\mathscr{S}(A)
, symmetries preserve polarity, and

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{\emph{(1)}}&\text{if $\theta\in\mathscr{S}_{+}(A)\cap\mathscr{S}_{-}(A)$,
then $\theta=\mathrm{id}_{x}$ for $x\in\mathscr{C}(A)$,}\\
\text{\emph{(2)}}&\text{if $\theta\in\mathscr{S}_{-}(A)$,
$\theta\subseteq^{-}\theta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}(A)$, then $\theta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{-}(A)$,}\\
\text{\emph{(3)}}&\text{if $\theta\in\mathscr{S}_{+}(A)$,
$\theta\subseteq^{+}\theta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}(A)$, then $\theta^{\prime}\in\mathscr{S}_{+}(A)$,}\end{array}

where
\theta\subseteq^{p}\theta^{\prime}
is
\theta\subseteq\theta^{\prime}
with (pairs of) events of polarity
p
.

Elements of
\mathscr{S}_{+}(A)
(resp.
\mathscr{S}_{-}(A)
) are called

positive
(resp.
negative
); they intuitively
correspond to symmetries carried by positive (resp. negative) moves,
and thus introduced by Player (resp. Opponent). We write
\theta:x\cong_{A}^{-}y
(resp.
\theta:x\cong_{A}^{+}y
) if
\theta\in\mathscr{S}_{-}(A)

(resp.
\theta\in\mathscr{S}_{+}(A)
).

Each symmetry has a unique positive-negative factorization
[
Cla24
, Lemma
7.1.18]
:

Lemma 15
.

For
A
a tcg and
\theta:x\cong_{A}z
, there are unique

y\in\mathscr{C}(A)
,
\theta_{-}:x\cong_{A}^{-}y
and
\theta_{+}:y\cong_{A}^{+}z
s.t.
\theta=\theta_{+}\circ\theta_{-}
.

We extend with symmetry the basic constructions on games:
the
dual

A^{\perp}
has the same symmetries as
A
, but

\mathscr{S}_{+}(A^{\perp})=\mathscr{S}_{-}(A)
and
\mathscr{S}_{-}(A^{\perp})=\mathscr{S}_{+}(A)
;
the
tensor

A_{1}\otimes A_{2}
has symmetries of the form
\theta_{1}\otimes\theta_{2}:x_{1}\otimes x_{2}\cong_{A_{1}\otimes A_{2}}y_{1}\otimes y_{2}
, where each

\theta_{i}:x_{i}\cong_{A_{i}}y_{i}
, and similarly for positive and
negative symmetries; the
hom

A\vdash B
is
A^{\perp}\otimes B
.

5.1.3.
Thin strategies

We now define strategies on thin
concurrent games:

{defi}

Consider
A
a tcg.

A
strategy
on
A
, written
\sigma:A
, is an ess
\sigma
equipped with a morphism of ess
\partial_{\sigma}:\sigma\to A
forming a strategy in the sense of Definition

4.1.2
, and such that:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{\emph{(1)}}&\text{if $\theta\in\mathscr{S}(\sigma),\partial_{\sigma}\theta\vdash_{A}(a^{-},b^{-})$, there are unique $\theta\vdash_{\sigma}(s,t)$ s.t. $\partial_{\sigma}s=a$ and
$\partial_{\sigma}t=b$.}\\
\text{\emph{(2)}}&\text{if $\theta:x\cong_{\sigma}y,\partial_{\sigma}\theta\in\mathscr{S}_{+}(A)$, then $x=y$ and $\theta=\mathrm{id}_{x}$.}\end{array}

As before, a
strategy from
A
to
B
is a strategy on
\sigma:A\vdash B
.

The first condition forces
\sigma
to acknowledge Opponent symmetries
in
A
; the notation
\theta\vdash_{A}(a,b)

means
(a,b)\not\in\theta
and
\theta\cup\{(a,b)\}\in\mathscr{S}(A)
. The second condition is
thinness
: it means that any
non-identity symmetry in the strategy must originate from Opponent.

5.1.4.
Comparison with the “saturated” approach

The “thin” approach is only one possible way of adding symmetry to
games. Other models
(e.g.
[
BDER97a
,
CCW14
,
Mel19
]
)
follow a different approach, where strategies satisfy a

saturation
condition.

We explain the difference in the language of concurrent games.
Consider a strategy
\sigma:A
on a tcg, in the sense of
Definition
5.1.3

without
conditions (1) and (2).
The saturation condition
[
CCW14
]
corresponds to a fibration property of the
functor

\partial_{\sigma}:\mathscr{S}(\sigma)\to\mathscr{S}(A)
: for
x\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma)
and
\psi:\partial_{\sigma}x\cong_{A}y
, there is a unique
\varphi:x\cong_{\sigma}z
such that
\partial_{\sigma}\varphi=\psi
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\mathscr{S}(\sigma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\mathscr{S}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{\sigma}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\partial_{\sigma}}

(7)

In contrast, thin strategies satisfy a different lifting property:
a unique lifting exists, up to a positive symmetry
[
Cla24
, Lemma
7.2.6]
:

Lemma 16
.

Let
\sigma:A
be a strategy as in Definition
5.1.3
.
For all
x\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma)
and
\psi:\partial_{\sigma}x\cong_{A}y
,
there are unique
\varphi:x\cong_{\sigma}z
and
\theta^{+}:\partial_{\sigma}z\cong_{A}^{+}y
such that
\theta^{+}\circ\partial^{\sigma}\varphi=\psi
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\mathscr{S}(\sigma)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\mathscr{S}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{\sigma}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{\sigma}z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\partial_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{\sigma}\varphi}

Proof 5.1

(Sketch)
.

Existence
is proved first for
\psi
positive, by induction on

x
using condition
(1)
of Definition
5.1.3
and
properties of isomorphism families; and then generalized to arbitrary

\psi
.
Uniqueness
follows from condition
(2)
of
Definition
5.1.3
.

Below, we will use this to construct a distributor from a
thin strategy. We note that saturated strategies are closer to
distributors, because the saturation property (
7
)
directly induces a functorial action of the groupoid
\mathscr{S}(A)
.
However, saturated strategies are more difficult to
understand operationally, and have not achieved the precision
of thin strategies for languages with state, concurrency, or non-determinism.

5.2.
From strategies to distributors

For tcgs
A
and
B
, we show how to construct

\|\sigma\|:\mathscr{S}(A)^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{S}(B)\to\mathbf{Set}

a distributor
from a strategy
\sigma:A\vdash B
. The key idea is to use
witnesses “up to positive symmetry” and use
the lifting
property in Lemma
16
.

For
x_{A}\in\mathscr{C}(A)
and
x_{B}\in\mathscr{C}(B)
we define the set of

positive witnesses
of
(x_{A},x_{B})
, written

\|\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{B})
, as the set of all triples
(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{+})
such that
x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\sigma)
and

\theta_{A}^{-}:x_{A}{\cong_{A}^{-}}x^{\sigma}_{A}\,\qquad\qquad\theta_{B}^{+}:x^{\sigma}_{B}{\cong_{B}^{+}}x_{B}

are positive symmetries on
A^{\perp}
and
B
.
The groupoid actions of
A
and
B
on this set
are determined by the uniqueness result:

Proposition 17
.

Consider
(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{+})\in\|\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{B})
.

For each
\Omega_{A}:y_{A}\cong_{A}x_{A}
and
\Omega_{B}:x_{B}\cong_{B}y_{B}
,
there are unique
\varphi:x^{\sigma}\cong_{\sigma}y^{\sigma}
and

\vartheta_{A}^{-}:y_{A}\cong_{A}^{-}x^{\sigma}_{A}
,
\vartheta_{B}^{-}:y^{\sigma}_{B}\cong_{B}^{+}y_{B}
such that the following two diagrams commute:

\textstyle{x_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{A}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{A}}
\textstyle{x^{\sigma}_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\sigma}_{A}}
\textstyle{y_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{A}^{-}}
\textstyle{y^{\sigma}_{A}}

\textstyle{x^{\sigma}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{B}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\sigma}_{B}}
\textstyle{x_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega_{B}}
\textstyle{y^{\sigma}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{B}^{+}}
\textstyle{y_{B}}

Proof 5.2
.

Existence
by Lem.
16
,

uniqueness
by
(2)
of Definition
5.1.3
.

Thus we may set
\|\sigma\|(\Omega_{A},\Omega_{B})(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{+})
as the positive witness
(\vartheta_{A}^{-},y^{\sigma},\vartheta_{B}^{+})
above. This immediately gives us a
distributor:

Proposition 18
.

We have
\|\sigma\|:\mathscr{S}(A)^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{S}(B)\to\mathbf{Set}
.

5.3.
The double category of thin concurrent games

We now define the double category
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
of thin concurrent
games. We have already defined the objects (tcgs, Definition
5.1.2
)
and the horizontal morphisms (thin strategies,
Definition
5.1.3
), so it remains to define the vertical
morphisms and
2
-cells, and appropriate compositions and identities.

5.3.1.
Vertical morphisms: maps between tcgs.

Recall that in
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}
the vertical morphisms between games are maps
of event structures preserving the polarity of moves. Here we define

maps of tcgs

A\to B
to be maps of event structures with
symmetry, preserving the polarity of moves, and preserving the polarity of symmetry
bijections.

5.3.2.
2-cells: morphisms of strategies

We now define
generalized morphisms between strategies. The 2-cells of
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
are more liberal than those in
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}
, because
there should be an isomorphism between two strategies which play
symmetric – rather than equal – moves. Recall the
2-dimensional structure in
\mathbf{ESS}
, given by the equivalence relation

\sim
on morphisms (§
5.1.1
). For two maps
f,g:E\to A
into a tcg, we write
f\sim^{+}g
if
f\sim g
and for every
x\in\mathscr{C}(E)
the symmetry obtained as the composition

f\,x\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f^{-1}}}{{\simeq}}\quad x\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\simeq}}\quad g\,x\,,

witnessing
f\sim g
for
x
, is positive.

{defi}

Let
\sigma:A\vdash B
and
\tau:C\vdash D
be thin
strategies, and let
h:A\to C
and
k:B\to D
be maps of tcgs. A

positive morphism of strategies
of type

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

is a rigid map of ess
f:\sigma\to\tau
s.t. the following diagram commutes up to
positive symmetry:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\vdash B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\vdash D}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\vdash k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim^{+}}

i.e.

\partial_{\tau}\circ f\sim^{+}(h\vdash k)\circ\partial_{\sigma}
.

As a convention, if
f
is a 2-cell as above, for

x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma)
we write

f[x^{\sigma}]:(h\vdash k)(\partial_{\sigma}x^{\sigma})\cong_{A}^{+}\partial_{\tau}(f\,x^{\sigma})

for the positive symmetry witnessing this.

5.3.3.
Composition and identity

The composition of thin
strategies
\sigma:A\vdash B
and
\tau:B\vdash C
is obtained by
equipping
\tau\odot\sigma
(Proposition

9
) with an adequate isomorphism family. If

\mathscr{S}^{+}(\sigma)
is the restriction of
\mathscr{S}(\sigma)
to

+
-covered configurations, then we can write
\mathbf{CC}(\mathscr{S}^{+}(\sigma),\mathscr{S}^{+}(\tau))

for the pairs
(\varphi^{\sigma},\varphi^{\tau})
of symmetries
which are matching, i.e.
\varphi^{\sigma}_{B}=\varphi^{\tau}_{B}
and whose
domain (and necessarily, codomain) are causally compatible.

Proposition 19
.

Consider
\sigma:A\vdash B
and
\tau:B\vdash C

thin strategies.

There is a unique symmetry on
\tau\odot\sigma
with a bijection
commuting with
\mathrm{dom}
and
\mathrm{cod}

(-\odot-):\mathbf{CC}(\mathscr{S}^{+}(\sigma),\mathscr{S}^{+}(\tau))\simeq\mathscr{S}^{+}(\tau\odot\sigma)

and compatible with
display maps,
i.e.

(\varphi^{\tau}\odot\varphi^{\sigma})_{A}=\varphi^{\sigma}_{A}
and

(\varphi^{\tau}\odot\varphi^{\sigma})_{C}=\varphi^{\tau}_{C}
.

Proof 5.3
.

This follows from
[
Cla24
, Proposition 7.3.1]
.

Most of the effort in organizing these components into a double
category is due to the difficulty of composing 2-cells
horizontally. Suppose we have a pair of 2-cells
f
and
g
as in (
6
),
but where all components now have symmetry. Explicitly we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\vdash B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\vdash B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{\sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sim^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{\sigma^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h\vdash k}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\tau^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\vdash C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B^{\prime}\vdash C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{\tau}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sim^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{\tau^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{k\vdash l}}

and we must define an appropriate map
g\odot f:\tau\odot\sigma\to\tau^{\prime}\odot\sigma^{\prime}
.

Recall (from the discussion following Theorem
10
) that
in the analogous situation in
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}
,

g\odot f:\tau\odot\sigma\Rightarrow\tau^{\prime}\odot\sigma^{\prime}
was
characterised by

(g\odot f)(x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma})=g(x^{\tau})\odot f(x^{\sigma})
. In

\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
this simple description is no longer possible, as we may not
have a matching situation
f(x^{\sigma})_{B^{\prime}}=g(x^{\tau})_{B^{\prime}}
. Instead,
these two configurations are matching up to a symmetry

\theta^{f,g}_{x^{\sigma},x^{\tau}}:f(x^{\sigma})_{B^{\prime}}\cong_{B^{\prime}}g(x^{\tau})_{B^{\prime}}

obtained as
\theta^{f,g}_{x^{\sigma},x^{\tau}}=g[x^{\tau}]_{B^{\prime}}\circ f[x^{\sigma}]_{B^{\prime}}^{-1}
.
Fortunately, interaction of thin strategies supports a notion of
synchronisation
up to symmetry

[
Cla24
, Proposition 7.4.4]
:

Proposition 20
.

Consider
x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\sigma),\theta_{B}:x^{\sigma}_{B}\cong_{B}x^{\tau}_{B},x^{\tau}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\tau)

causally compatible
,

i.e.

the relation
\vartriangleleft
induced on the graph of the composite bijection

x^{\sigma}\parallel x^{\tau}_{C}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\sigma}\parallel x^{\tau}_{C}}}{{\simeq}}\!x^{\sigma}_{A}\parallel x^{\sigma}_{B}\parallel x^{\tau}_{C}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x^{\sigma}_{A}\parallel\theta\parallel x^{\tau}_{C}}}{{\simeq}}\!x^{\sigma}_{A}\parallel x^{\tau}_{B}\parallel x^{\tau}_{C}\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x^{\sigma}_{A}\parallel\partial_{\tau}^{-1}}}{{\simeq}}\!x^{\sigma}_{A}\parallel x^{\tau}

by
<_{\sigma\parallel C}
and
<_{A\parallel\tau}

as in §
4.2.2
, is acyclic – we also say the
composite bijection is
secured
.

Then, there are unique
y^{\tau}\odot y^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\tau\odot\sigma)
with symmetries

\varphi^{\sigma}:x^{\sigma}\cong_{\sigma}y^{\sigma}
and
\varphi^{\tau}:x^{\tau}\cong_{\tau}y^{\tau}
, such that
\varphi^{\sigma}_{A}\in\mathscr{S}_{-}(A)

and
\varphi^{\tau}_{C}\in\mathscr{S}_{+}(C)
, and
\varphi^{\tau}_{B}\circ\theta=\varphi^{\sigma}_{B}
.

In that case, we write
y^{\tau}\odot y^{\sigma}=x^{\tau}\odot_{\theta}x^{\sigma}
. With that notation, there is a unique positive morphism

g\odot f:\tau\odot\sigma\Rightarrow\tau^{\prime}\odot\sigma^{\prime}
s.t.

(g\odot f)(x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma})=g(x^{\tau})\odot_{\theta^{f,g}_{x^{\sigma},x^{\tau}}}f(x^{\sigma})
. This serves as a
definition of the horizontal composition of
2
-cells, see

[
Paq20
]
or
[
Cla24
, Theorem 7.4.13]
.

Finally, for the identity horizontal morphism in
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
, we equip the
copycat strategy
\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}:A\vdash A
(Proposition

8
) with the unique symmetry that has an iso

\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{(-)}:\mathscr{S}(A)\simeq\mathscr{S}^{+}(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A})

commuting with
\mathrm{dom}
and
\mathrm{cod}
, such that

\partial_{\mathbf{\hskip 1.55554ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}}(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{\theta})=\theta\vdash\theta
.

The tensor product
\otimes
of tcgs extends to a symmetric monoidal
structure. We omit all of the details, as the situation is completely
analogous to that in
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}
, using that the symmetry is always
handled componentwise in a tensor product of games. The following
proposition assembles all of the structure defined in this section.

Proposition 21
.

There is a symmetric monoidal double category
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
, and a
symmetric monoidal embedding
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
.

Thus we have extended our basic model
\mathbb{C}\mathbf{G}
with symmetries. The
new model supports an exponential modality, which we discuss in
§
5.5
.

5.4.
An oplax functor
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}\to\mathbb{D}\mathbf{ist}

We now give the components of an oplax double functor
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}\to\mathbb{D}\mathbf{ist}
. We have already explained the action on objects and
horizontal morphisms,
by mapping every strategy to a distributor
(§
5.2
). We turn to the action on vertical
morphisms and 2-cells.

5.4.1.
From maps of tcgs to functors of groupoids.

If
A
and

B
are tcgs, and
l:A\to B
is a map preserving symmetries, then
the action of
l
on configurations and symmetries determines a functor

\|l\|:\mathscr{S}(A)\to\mathscr{S}(B)
.

5.4.2.
From positive morphisms to natural transformations

We must show that a 2-cell in
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
, say
f
in the diagram below,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\vdash B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\vdash D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{\sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sim^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\partial_{\tau}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{h\vdash k}}

determines a natural transformation of distributors

\|\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{B})\Rightarrow\|\tau\|(h(x_{A}),k(x_{B}))
. Its components are the functions

\begin{array}[]{rcrcl}\|f\|_{x_{A},x_{B}}&\!\!\!\!\!\!:&\!\!\|\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{B})&\to&\|\tau\|(h(x_{A}),k(x_{B}))\\
&&(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{+})&\!\!\!\!\!\!\mapsto&\!\!(\theta^{x}_{C}\circ h(\theta_{A}^{-}),f(x^{\sigma}),k(\theta_{B}^{+})\circ{\theta^{x}_{D}}^{-1})\end{array}

for
x_{A}\in\mathscr{C}(A)
and
x_{B}\in\mathscr{C}(B)
, where

\theta^{x}_{C}:h(x^{\sigma}_{A})\cong_{C}^{-}f(x^{\sigma})_{C}
and

\theta^{x}_{D}:k(x^{\sigma}_{B})\cong_{D}^{+}f(x^{\sigma})_{D}
are determined by
the commutative diagram above, by definition of
\sim^{+}
(see
§
5.3
).
This is natural, as an application of Proposition
17
.

5.4.3.
The unitor and compositor

To complete the construction of the double functor
\|-\|
, we must
define appropriate globular 2-cells. This double functor will be

oplax normal
, meaning that there are invertible
unitors
and
non-invertible
compositors
. We define these in the next two
propositions.

Proposition 22
.

Consider
A
a tcg. Then, there is a natural iso

\mathsf{pid}^{A}:\|\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\Rightarrow}}\mathscr{S}(A)[-,-]:\mathscr{S}(A)^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{S}(A)\to\mathbf{Set}\,.

Proof 5.4
.

Consider
(\theta^{-},\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{z},\theta^{+})\in\|\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}\|(x,y)
, with

\theta^{-}:x\cong_{A}^{-}z
and
\theta^{+}:z\cong_{A}^{+}y
. We
set
\mathsf{pid}^{A}(\theta^{-},\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{z},\theta^{+})=\theta^{+}\circ\theta^{-}
;
naturality and invertibility follow from Lemma

15
.

Likewise, for two strategies
\sigma:A\vdash B
and
\tau:B\vdash C
, we have a compositor as follows.

Proposition 23
.

For any
\sigma:A\vdash B
and
\tau:B\vdash C
, there is a
natural transformation:

\mathsf{pcomp}^{\sigma,\tau}:\|\tau\odot\sigma\|\Rightarrow\|\tau\|\bullet\|\sigma\|:\mathscr{S}(A)^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{S}(B)\to\mathbf{Set}\,.

Proof 5.5
.

Consider
(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma},\theta_{C}^{+})\in\|\tau\odot\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{C})
; this is sent by

\mathsf{pcomp}^{\sigma,\tau}_{x_{A},x_{C}}
to (the equivalence
class for the equivalence
\sim
originating for the coend formula
(
4
) of) the pair

((\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}}),(\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}},x^{\tau},\theta_{C}^{+}))\in(\|\tau\|\bullet\|\sigma\|)(x_{A},x_{C})

for
x^{\sigma}_{B}=x^{\tau}_{B}=x_{B}
, exploiting that
x^{\sigma}
and

x^{\tau}
are matching.

We must show that the function
\mathsf{pcomp}^{\sigma,\tau}(x_{A},x_{C}):\|\tau\odot\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{C})\to(\|\tau\|\bullet\|\sigma\|)(x_{A},x_{C})
is natural in
x_{A},x_{C}
.
Consider
\mathsf{w}^{\tau\odot\sigma}\in\|\tau\odot\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{C})
,
written as

\mathsf{w}^{\tau\odot\sigma}=(\psi_{A}^{-},x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma},\psi_{C}^{+})\,,

hence with
\mathsf{pcomp}(\mathsf{w}^{\tau\odot\sigma})=(\mathsf{w}^{\sigma},\mathsf{w}^{\tau})

where
\mathsf{w}^{\sigma}=(\psi_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}_{x^{\sigma}_{B}})
and

\mathsf{w}^{\tau}=(\mathrm{id}_{x^{\tau}_{B}},x^{\tau},\psi_{C}^{+})
.

Now consider
\theta_{A}:y_{A}\cong_{A}x_{A},\theta_{C}:x_{C}\cong_{C}y_{C}
,
then by definition of
\theta_{C}\cdot\mathsf{w}^{\tau\odot\sigma}\cdot\theta_{A}
,
it must be given as
(\nu_{A}^{-},y^{\tau}\odot y^{\sigma},\nu_{C}^{+})
as in
the bottom of the following diagram:

\textstyle{x_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{A}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{A}^{-1}}
\textstyle{x^{\sigma}_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\sigma}_{A}}
\textstyle{x^{\sigma}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\sigma}_{B}}
\textstyle{x_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

=

\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{x^{\tau}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\tau}_{B}}
\textstyle{x^{\tau}_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\tau}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{C}^{+}}
\textstyle{x_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{C}}
\textstyle{y_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{A}^{-}}
\textstyle{y^{\sigma}_{A}}
\textstyle{y^{\sigma}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{y_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{y^{\tau}_{B}}
\textstyle{y^{\tau}_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{C}^{+}}
\textstyle{y_{C}}

for
\varphi^{\sigma}:x^{\sigma}\cong_{\sigma}y^{\sigma}
and

\varphi^{\tau}:x^{\tau}\cong_{\tau}y^{\tau}
. But it also follows

\mathsf{w}^{\sigma}\cdot\theta_{A}=(\nu_{A}^{-},y^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}_{y^{\sigma}_{B}})\,,\qquad\theta_{C}\cdot\mathsf{w}^{\tau}=(\mathrm{id}_{y^{\tau}_{B}},y^{\tau},\nu_{C}^{+})

by definition of these functorial actions, so that

\mathsf{pcomp}(\theta_{C}\cdot\mathsf{w}^{\tau\odot\sigma}\cdot\theta_{A})=(\mathsf{w}^{\sigma}\cdot\theta_{A},\theta_{C}\cdot\mathsf{w}^{\tau})

as required for the naturality of
\mathsf{pcomp}^{\sigma,\tau}
.

Next, we must additionally prove that
\mathsf{pcomp}^{\sigma,\tau}
is
natural in
\sigma
and
\tau
:

Lemma 24

(Naturality of
\mathsf{pcomp}^{\sigma,\tau}
)

Proof 5.6
.

Consider
\mathsf{w}^{\tau\odot\sigma}=(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma},\theta_{C}^{+})\in\|\tau\odot\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{C})
. We know that

\partial_{\sigma^{\prime}}\circ f\sim^{+}(h\vdash k)\partial_{\sigma}\,,

which unfolds to mean that for each
x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma)
, the
composite bijection

(h\vdash k)(\partial_{\sigma}\,x^{\sigma})\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h\vdash k}}{{\simeq}}\quad\partial_{\sigma}\,x^{\sigma}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\sigma}}}{{\simeq}}\quad x^{\sigma}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\simeq}}\quad f(x^{\sigma})\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\sigma^{\prime}}}}{{\simeq}}\quad\partial_{\sigma^{\prime}}(f(x^{\sigma}))

is a positive symmetry in
A^{\prime}\vdash B^{\prime}
. In other words, there is a
negative symmetry
f[x^{\sigma}]_{A^{\prime}}
and a positive symmetry

f[x^{\sigma}]_{B^{\prime}}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{x^{\sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{x^{\sigma}_{A}\vdash x^{\sigma}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h\vdash k}
\textstyle{h(x^{\sigma}_{A})\vdash k(x^{\sigma}_{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f[x^{\sigma}]_{A^{\prime}}\vdash f[x^{\sigma}]_{B^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{f(x^{\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{\sigma^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{f(x^{\sigma})_{A^{\prime}}\vdash f(x^{\sigma})_{B^{\prime}}}

and we have an analogous symmetry induced by the 2-cell
g
. By Proposition

20
, there are unique
\varphi^{\sigma^{\prime}},\varphi^{\tau^{\prime}}
and
\vartheta_{A^{\prime}}^{-},\vartheta_{C^{\prime}}^{+}
s.t.

\textstyle{f(x^{\sigma})_{A^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{A^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{f(x^{\sigma})_{B^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{B^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{f[x^{\sigma}]_{B^{\prime}}^{-1}}
\textstyle{k(x_{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g[x^{\tau}]_{B^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{g(x^{\tau})_{B^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\tau^{\prime}}_{B^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{g(x^{\tau})_{C^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\tau^{\prime}}_{C^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{g[x^{\tau}]_{C^{\prime}}^{-1}}
\textstyle{h(x_{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f[x^{\sigma}]_{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{A^{\prime}}^{-}}
\textstyle{l(x_{C})}
\textstyle{y^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{A^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{y^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{B^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{y_{B^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{y^{\tau^{\prime}}_{B^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{y^{\tau^{\prime}}_{C^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{C^{\prime}}^{+}}

commutes (the line on the top is secured since
f,g
are rigid); and
by definition
g\odot f:\tau\odot\sigma\to\tau^{\prime}\odot\sigma^{\prime}

is the unique map such that
(g\odot f)(x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma})=y^{\tau^{\prime}}\odot y^{\sigma^{\prime}}
. Thus

\mathsf{pcomp}^{\sigma^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}}\circ\|g\odot f\|(\mathsf{w}^{\tau\odot\sigma})=((\vartheta_{A}^{-},y^{\sigma^{\prime}},\mathrm{id}),(\mathrm{id},y^{\tau^{\prime}},\vartheta_{C}^{+}))\,.

Now, likewise, we have

\displaystyle\|f\|(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}_{x^{\sigma}_{B}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(f[x^{\sigma}]_{A^{\prime}}\circ h(\theta_{A}^{-}),f(x^{\sigma}),f[x^{\sigma}]_{B^{\prime}}^{-1})

\displaystyle\|g\|(\mathrm{id}_{x^{\tau}_{B}},x^{\tau},\theta_{C}^{+})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(g[x^{\tau}]_{B^{\prime}},g(x^{\tau}),l(\theta_{C}^{+})\circ g[x^{\tau}]_{C^{\prime}}^{-1})

but by the diagram above, writing
\Theta_{B^{\prime}}=\varphi^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{B^{\prime}}\circ f[x^{\sigma}]_{B^{\prime}}=\varphi^{\tau^{\prime}}_{B^{\prime}}\circ g[x^{\tau}]_{B^{\prime}}
, we have the
equalities

\displaystyle(\vartheta_{A^{\prime}}^{-},y^{\sigma^{\prime}},\mathrm{id}_{y_{B^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Theta_{B^{\prime}}\cdot((f[x^{\sigma}]_{A^{\prime}}\circ h(\theta_{A^{\prime}}^{-}),f(x^{\sigma}),f[x^{\sigma}]_{B^{\prime}}^{-1})

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{y_{B^{\prime}}},y^{\tau^{\prime}},\vartheta_{C^{\prime}}^{+})\cdot\Theta_{B^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(g[x^{\tau}]_{B^{\prime}},g(x^{\tau}),l(\theta_{C}^{+})\circ g[x^{\tau}]_{C^{\prime}}^{-1})

so that we may now compute

\displaystyle((\vartheta_{A^{\prime}}^{-},y^{\sigma^{\prime}},\mathrm{id}),(\mathrm{id},y^{\tau^{\prime}},\vartheta_{C^{\prime}}^{+}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\Theta_{B^{\prime}}\cdot(f[x^{\sigma}]_{A^{\prime}}\circ h(\theta_{A}^{-}),f(x^{\sigma}),f[x^{\sigma}]_{B^{\prime}}^{-1}),(\mathrm{id},y^{\tau^{\prime}},\vartheta_{C^{\prime}}^{+}))

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle((f[x^{\sigma}]_{A^{\prime}}\circ h(\theta_{A}^{-}),f(x^{\sigma}),f[x^{\sigma}]_{B^{\prime}}^{-1}),(\mathrm{id},y^{\tau^{\prime}},\vartheta_{C^{\prime}}^{+})\cdot\Theta_{B^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((f[x^{\sigma}]_{A^{\prime}}\circ h(\theta_{A}^{-}),f(x^{\sigma}),f[x^{\sigma}]_{B^{\prime}}^{-1}),(g[x^{\tau}]_{B^{\prime}},g(x^{\tau}),l(\theta_{C}^{+})\circ g[x^{\tau}]_{C^{\prime}}^{-1}))

as required to establish the desired commutation.

Equipped with the above data, the operation
\|-\|
is oplax-functorial:

Theorem 25
.

The operation
\|-\|:\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}\to\mathbb{D}\mathbf{ist}
defines a symmetric monoidal, oplax double functor.

Proof 5.7
.

There are three coherence diagrams to check for functoriality, involving only globular 2-cells. The preservation of
the associator is straightforward. For the preservation of the
unitor, we establish that the diagram of globular 2-cells

\textstyle{\|\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{B}\odot\sigma\|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{pcomp}}
\textstyle{\|\sigma\|}
\textstyle{\|\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{B}\|\bullet\|\sigma\|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{pid}\bullet\|\sigma\|}
\textstyle{\mathrm{id}_{B}\bullet\|\sigma\|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{\|\sigma\|}}

commutes for any
\sigma:A\vdash B
. For this, consider

\mathsf{w}=(\theta_{A}^{-},\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x^{\sigma}_{B}}\odot x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{+})\in\|\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{B}\odot\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{B})
.
We have
\mathsf{pcomp}(\mathsf{w})=((\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}),(\mathrm{id},\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x^{\sigma}_{B}},\theta_{B}^{+}))
, sent by
\mathsf{pid}\bullet\|\sigma\|
to
((\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}),\theta_{B}^{+})
. Now

\displaystyle((\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}),\theta_{B}^{+})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}),\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}}\cdot\theta_{B}^{+})

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle(\theta_{B}^{+}\cdot(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}_{x^{\sigma}_{B}}),\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{+}),\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}})

satisfying
\rho((\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{+}),\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}})=(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{+})
as required. The other coherence
diagram for the unitor is symmetric.

We must additionally show that the double functor has a symmetric
monoidal structure. All the necessary data is defined in the obvious
way: we have a vertical transformation
consisting of vertical isomorphisms

\|A\|\otimes\|B\|\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\|A\otimes B\|

and invertible 2-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\|A\|\otimes\|B\|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\|A^{\prime}\|\otimes\|B^{\prime}\|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\|A\otimes B\|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\|A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}\|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|\sigma\|\otimes\|\tau\|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|\sigma\otimes\tau\|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

as well as a vertical isomorphism
I\cong\|I\|
(we use
the same
I
for monoidal units in different categories) and an invertible 2-cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\|I\|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\|I\|}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|\mathbf{\hskip 1.55554ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{I}\|}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

These must satisfy a small number of coherence axioms (see

[
GGV24
, Def. 5.1]
), which are all immediately verified.

At this point, we have defined an oplax double functor preserving the
monoidal structure appropriately. This is not a pseudo double functor:
in general, the compositor 2-cell is not invertible, as witnessed in
the example of Figure
1
. We will address this below in
§
6
, by specializing the games and strategies
we consider to ensure that no deadlock arises. Before that, we
discuss another orthogonal issue: the collapse above does not preserve
the exponential modality
\oc
.

5.5.
Difficulties with the exponential modality

First, we recall how to construct an exponential modality on
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
.

5.5.1.
An exponential modality in polarized
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}

For an ess
E
, the ess
\oc E
is an infinitary symmetric tensor
product, where the elements of the indexing set are called
copy indices
:

{defi}

Consider
E
an ess.
Then
\oc E
has:
events
,
|\oc E|=\mathbb{N}\times|E|
;
causality and conflict
inherited transparently.
The
isomorphism family

\mathscr{S}(\oc E)
comprises all bijections

\theta:~\parallel_{i\in\mathbb{N}}x_{i}\simeq~\parallel_{i\in\mathbb{N}}y_{i}

between configurations
such that there is a bijection
\pi:\mathbb{N}\simeq\mathbb{N}
and
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, a symmetry
\theta_{i}:x_{i}\cong_{A}y_{\pi(i)}
, such that for every
(j,a)\in|\oc E|
, we have

\theta(j,a)=(\pi(j),\theta_{j}(a))
.

To extend this to tcgs, we must treat separately the positive and negative
symmetries. We explained earlier that intuitively, symmetries that only
change the copy indices of negative moves should be negative, and
likewise for positive moves – but this naive definition does not yield
a tcg in general
[
CCW19
]
. To obtain a
sensible extension of
\oc
to tcgs, We must
restrict to a polarized setting in which tcgs are
negative
,
meaning that all minimal events are negative. For a negative tcg
A
, a
symmetry
\theta\in\mathscr{S}(\oc A)
is in the sub-family
\mathscr{S}_{-}(\oc A)
if each
\theta_{i}
in Definition
5.5.1
is negative in

\mathscr{S}(A)
, whereas
\theta
is in
\mathscr{S}_{+}(\oc A)
if each
\theta_{i}

is in
\mathscr{S}_{+}(A)
and
additionally

\pi
is the identity
bijection. This extends to a horizontal double comonad on the sub-double-category
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}^{-}

of
negative
tcgs – this is detailed for instance in

[
Paq20
]
.

5.5.2.
Our functor does not preserve the modality

However, in this paper we shall not adopt that exact construction,
because that exponential modality
\oc
on
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}^{-}
is
not

preserved by the oplax functor of Theorem
25
. We
illustrate with an example:

{exa}

Consider
I
the empty tcg.

Then, the two groupoids
\mathscr{S}(\oc I)
and
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{S}(I))
are not
equivalent in general. Indeed,
\oc I
is still empty so that

\mathscr{S}(\oc I)
is a singleton groupoid. In contrast,
\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{S}(I))

includes

\emptyset,~\emptyset\emptyset,~\emptyset\emptyset\emptyset,\dots\,,

i.e.
countably many non-isomorphic objects.

Intuitively, static models such as
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{ist}
record how many
times we “do nothing”, whereas
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
only records when we do
something.
Thus, although one can construct a cartesian closed Kleisli bicategory
from the restriction of
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
to negative games

[
Paq20
]
, the functor
\|-\|
will not
preserve cartesian closed structure.

We shall resolve this in §
7
by adopting a
notion of games where not all configurations are considered “valid”
and correspond to a point of the web in the relational model. Before we
do that, let us address the oplax aspect of our collapse.

6.
Visible strategies and a pseudofunctor

A fundamental difference between dynamic and static models is the
ability for dynamic models to detect deadlocks, via the causal
nature of strategies. This situation is encapsulated in the “oplax-ness” of
the double functor
\|-\|:\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}\to\mathbb{D}\mathbf{ist}
. In this section,
we show how to restrict
the games and strategies so that deadlocks never occur, which resolves
the mismatch and gives a
pseudo
double functor, that preserves
composition strictly.

To perform this restriction, we import from
[
Cla24
]
the mechanism
of
visibility
. This gives a new double category
\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}
. In
this new setting, the symmetric monoidal oplax double functor of
Theorem
25
becomes a symmetric monoidal pseudo double
functor
\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}\to\mathbb{D}\mathbf{ist}
. The restriction to
\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}
is significant, e.g. the
game model of mutable state

[
CC21
,
Cla24
]
is no longer included, but
we retain a model of the
\lambda
-calculus.

6.1.
The double category
\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}

We first introduce the restricted double category
\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}
.

Games and strategies in
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
are very general, and entirely
independent of the specific computational paradigms they represent. In
contrast, in
\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}
, all games are close to those obtained by the
interpretation of simple types, and strategies are somewhat close to
those needed to model
\lambda
-terms. (We do have a bit more: for
instance,
\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}
supports pure parallel higher-order computation

[
CCW15
]
.)

6.1.1.
Arenas

The objects of our refined model are called
arenas
. Arenas
narrow down the causal structure to an
alternating forest
,
required for the definition of visible strategies.

{defi}

An
arena
is a tcg
A

such that

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{\emph{(1)}}&\text{if $a_{1},a_{2}\leq_{A}a_{3}$ then $a_{1}\leq_{A}a_{2}$ or $a_{2}\leq_{A}a_{1}$,}\\
\text{\emph{(2)}}&\text{if $a_{1}\rightarrowtriangle_{A}a_{2}$, then $\mathrm{pol}_{A}(a_{1})\neq\mathrm{pol}_{A}(a_{2})$}.\end{array}

Moreover,
A
is called a
-
-arena
if
A
is negative as a
tcg.

The dual, tensor and hom operations on tcgs preserve arenas.

A key consequence of this definition is that any non-minimal
a\in A

has a unique causal predecessor, called its
justifier
, and
denoted
\mathsf{j}(a)\in A
with
\mathsf{j}(a)\rightarrowtriangle_{A}a
.

6.1.2.
Visible strategies

Visibility captures a property of purely-functional parallel programs,
in which threads may fork and join but each should be a well-formed
stand-alone sequential execution.
In an event structure
E
, a thread is
formalized as a
grounded causal chain (gcc)
,
i.e.

a finite set
\rho\subseteq_{f}|E|
on which
\leq_{E}
is a total
order, forming a sequence

\rho_{1}\rightarrowtriangle_{E}\dots\rightarrowtriangle_{E}\rho_{n}

where
\rho_{1}
is minimal in
E
. We write
\mathsf{gcc}(E)
for the set of gccs. A gcc
need not be a configuration (although it will always be if the strategy
interprets a sequential program). A strategy is

visible
if gccs only reach valid states of the arena:

{defi}

[
[
CCW15
,
Cla24
]
]
Consider
A
an arena. Then
\sigma:A
is
visible
if it is:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{\emph{pointed:}}&\text{for any $s\in\sigma$, there is a unique $\mathsf{init}(s)\leq_{\sigma}s$ minimal in $\sigma$, which is negative,}\\
\text{\emph{valid-gccs:}}&\text{for all $\rho\in\mathsf{gcc}(\sigma)$, $\partial_{\sigma}(\rho)\in\mathscr{C}(A)$.}\end{array}

A key consequence of this definition is that it equips all non-initial
moves of
\sigma
with a
justifier
, analogously to Hyland-Ong
games: if
s\in\sigma
is such that
\partial_{\sigma}\,s
is non-initial,
then
\mathsf{j}(s)
is the (unique)
s^{\prime}\in\sigma
such that

\partial_{\sigma}\,s^{\prime}\rightarrowtriangle_{A}\partial_{\sigma}\,s
; if
\partial_{\sigma}\,s
is initial
but positive, we set
\mathsf{j}(s)=\mathsf{init}(s)
. Then,
visibility

entails the following fact: for any gcc in
\sigma
of the form

\rho=\rho_{1}\rightarrowtriangle_{E}\dots\rightarrowtriangle_{E}\rho_{n}^{+}\,,

the justifier
\mathsf{j}(\rho_{n}^{+})
of
\rho_{n}^{+}
must appear in
\rho
–
this is clearly analogous to the notion of
visibility
from
Hyland-Ong games
[
HO00
]
, explaining the terminology.
The key property of visible strategies for us here is that their
composition is always deadlock-free
[
Cla24
, Lemma 10.4.8]
:

Lemma 26
.

Consider
A,B,C

-
-arenas,
\sigma:A\vdash B
and
\tau:B\vdash C
visible strategies,
x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}(\sigma)
and

x^{\tau}\in\mathscr{C}(\tau)
with a symmetry
\theta:x^{\sigma}_{B}\cong_{B}x^{\tau}_{B}
.

Then,
x^{\sigma},\theta,x^{\tau}
are causally compatible in the sense
of Proposition
20
.

Copycat strategies on
-
-arenas are visible, and visible strategies
are closed under composition
[
Cla24
]
, so we may consider the
sub-double-category of
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
over the
-
-arenas and the visible
strategies, containing all vertical morphisms and 2-cells between
them. We call this double category
\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}
, and as usual we set

\mathbf{Vis}=\mathbf{H}(\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is})
, the bicategory of
-
-arenas, visible
strategies, and (globular) positive morphisms.

6.1.3.
A pseudo double functor

By restriction of the components from Theorem

25
, we have
an oplax double functor

\|-\|:\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}\to\mathbb{D}\mathbf{ist}.

This is actually a pseudo double functor, in that the compositor is invertible:

Theorem 27
.

We have a pseudo double functor
\|-\|:\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}\to\mathbb{D}\mathbf{ist}
.

Proof 6.1
.

We must show that for all
x_{A}\in\mathscr{C}(A)
and
x_{C}\in\mathscr{C}(C)
,

\mathsf{pcomp}^{\sigma,\tau}(x_{A},x_{C})
is a bijection.

For surjectivity, consider

\begin{array}[]{rcrcl}\mathsf{w}^{\sigma}&=&(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{+})&\in&\|\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{B})\\
\mathsf{w}^{\tau}&=&(\theta_{B}^{-},x^{\tau},\theta_{C}^{+})&\in&\|\tau\|(x_{B},x_{C})\\
\end{array}

composable witnesses. By Lemma
26
,

(x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{-}\circ\theta_{B}^{+},x^{\tau})

is causally compatible. By Proposition
20
,
there
are unique
y^{\tau}\odot y^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\tau\odot\sigma)
along
with
\varphi^{\sigma},\varphi^{\tau},\vartheta_{A}^{-},\vartheta_{C}^{+}
such
that:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\sigma}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\sigma}_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\tau}_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x^{\tau}_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\sigma}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\sigma}_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\tau}_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y^{\tau}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\sigma}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\sigma}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{B}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{B}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\tau}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\tau}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{C}^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{A}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{A}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{C}^{+}}

which, writing
\Theta_{B}=\varphi^{\sigma}_{B}\circ{\theta_{B}^{+}}^{-1}=\varphi^{\tau}_{B}\circ\theta_{B}^{-}
, entails

\begin{array}[]{rcrcl}\mathsf{v}^{\sigma}&=&(\vartheta_{A}^{-},y^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}_{y_{B}})&=&\Theta_{B}\cdot(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\theta_{B}^{+})\\
\mathsf{v}^{\tau}&=&(\mathrm{id}_{y_{B}},y^{\tau},\vartheta_{C}^{+})&=&(\theta_{B}^{-},x^{\tau},\theta_{C}^{+})\cdot\Theta_{B}\end{array}

so
(\mathsf{v}^{\sigma},\mathsf{v}^{\tau})=(\Theta_{B}\cdot\mathsf{w}^{\sigma},\mathsf{v}^{\tau})\sim(\mathsf{w}^{\sigma},\mathsf{v}^{\tau}\cdot\Theta_{B})=(\mathsf{w}^{\sigma},\mathsf{w}^{\tau})
.
Now
(\mathsf{v}^{\sigma},\mathsf{v}^{\tau})=\mathsf{pcomp}^{\sigma,\tau}(\vartheta_{A}^{-},y^{\tau}\odot y^{\sigma},\vartheta_{C}^{+})
, showing surjectivity.

Now, for injectivity, consider two witnesses

(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma},\theta_{C}^{+})\in\|\tau\odot\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{C})\,,\qquad(\vartheta_{A}^{-},y^{\tau}\odot y^{\sigma},\vartheta_{C}^{+})\in\|\tau\odot\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{C})\,,

s.t.

\mathsf{pcomp}(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma},\theta_{C}^{+})\sim\mathsf{pcomp}(\vartheta_{A}^{-},y^{\tau}\odot y^{\sigma},\vartheta_{C}^{+})
,

i.e.
, there are components such that

\displaystyle((\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}),(\mathrm{id},x^{\tau},\theta_{C}^{+}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}),(\mathrm{id},y^{\tau},\vartheta_{C}^{+})\cdot\Theta_{B})

\displaystyle((\vartheta_{A}^{-},y^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}),(\mathrm{id},y^{\tau},\vartheta_{C}^{+}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\Theta_{B}\cdot(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}),(\mathrm{id},y^{\tau},\vartheta_{C}^{+}))

which by definition of the functorial action, means that

\textstyle{x^{\sigma}_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\sigma}_{A}}
\textstyle{x^{\sigma}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\sigma}_{B}}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{x_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{\Theta_{B}}
\textstyle{x^{\tau}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\tau}_{B}}
\textstyle{x^{\tau}_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\tau}_{C}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{C}^{+}}
\textstyle{x_{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{A}^{-}}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{A}^{-}}
\textstyle{x_{C}}
\textstyle{y^{\sigma}_{A}}
\textstyle{y^{\sigma}_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{y_{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{y^{\tau}_{B}}
\textstyle{y^{\tau}_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{C}^{+}}

commutes for some
\varphi^{\sigma}:x^{\sigma}\cong_{\sigma}y^{\sigma}

and
\psi^{\tau}:x^{\tau}\cong_{\tau}y^{\tau}
. So

\varphi^{\tau}\odot\varphi^{\sigma}:x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma}\cong_{\tau\odot\sigma}y^{\tau}\odot y^{\sigma}

has a positive display, hence is an identity symmetry by condition

(2)
of Definition
5.1.3
– thus from the diagram,

(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma},\theta_{C}^{+})=(\vartheta_{A}^{-},y^{\tau}\odot y^{\sigma},\vartheta_{C}^{+})

as needed to conclude.

6.2.
Symmetric monoidal structure.

Visible strategies are closed under the tensor product
\otimes
, and
so the symmetric monoidal structure in
\mathbb{T}\mathbf{CG}
restricts to

\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}
. Since all components restrict, we deduce that
\|-\|:\mathbb{V}\mathbf{is}\to\mathbb{D}\mathbf{ist}
is a symmetric monoidal (pseudo) double
functor.

Applying Proposition
2
, we obtain a symmetric
monoidal pseudofunctor between the induced horizontal bicategories.

Theorem 28
.

The horizontal restriction of the collapse double functor
\|-\|

is a symmetric monoidal pseudofunctor of bicategories
\|-\|:\mathbf{Vis}\to\mathbf{TCG}
.

We emphasize that this theorem makes essential use of the double
categorical aspects, which makes the 2-dimensional symmetric monoidal
structure manageable. Now that this is established, we can focus
directly on the bicategorical structure, as we move
towards applications in the semantics of the
\lambda
-calculus.

7.
A Cartesian Closed Pseudofunctor

The paper until this point has focused on the preservation of
symmetric monoidal structure by our collapse functor. In this section
we add further structure to
\mathbf{Vis}
to ensure the preservation of the
exponential modality, so that our collapse lifts to the Kleisli
bicategory; we then show that the corresponding pseudofunctor is
cartesian closed. This development is mostly
independent from the preservation of the linear monoidal structure
detailed above; mainly by lack of a mature bicategorical theory of
models of linear logic.

To ensure preservation of the exponential modality, we
must first address the mismatch identified in §
5.5
.
We shall do that now, by introducing a mechanism that identifies those
positions in games that correspond to points of the relational model.

7.1.
Payoff and Winning

The additional mechanism we import here
is inspired from Melliès
[
Mel05
]
; see also the
detailed construction in
[
Cla24
]
. As mentioned above, it helps in
ignoring intermediate configurations arising in games which are not

complete
,
i.e.
they do not correspond to a valid state in
relational models. In addition, this mechanism makes the ambiant games
model
linear
rather than
affine
; it forces strategies
to explore all available resources, solving another mismatch between
games and relational models.

7.1.1.
The bicategory
\mathbf{Wis}
.

Here we construct a new
bicategory of games and strategies, integrating both mechanisms of

visibility
and
winning
.

Games with payoff.

Our new notion of game is that of a
board
:

{defi}

A
board
is an arena
A
along with

\kappa_{A}:\mathscr{C}(A)\to\{-1,0,+1\}

a
payoff function
, such that this data satisfies the following
conditions:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{\emph{invariant:}}&\text{for all $\theta:x\cong_{A}y$, we have
$\kappa_{A}(x)=\kappa_{A}(y)$,}\\
\text{\emph{race-free:}}&\text{for all $a\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.83331pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.83331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 18.83331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!_{A}\,a^{\prime}$, we have
$\mathrm{pol}_{A}(a)=\mathrm{pol}_{A}(a^{\prime})$,}\end{array}

where
a\!\!\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.83331pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.83331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawsquiggles@}}{\hbox{\kern 18.83331pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\,}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!_{A}\,a^{\prime}
means an
immediate
conflict
,
i.e.

a\mathrel{\#}_{A}a^{\prime}
and it is not inherited.

A
-
-board
is additionally negative as an arena, and must also satisfy:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{\emph{initialized:}}&\kappa_{A}(\emptyset)\geq 0\,.\end{array}

Finally, a
-
-board
A
is
strict
if

\kappa_{A}(\emptyset)=1
and all its initial moves are in pairwise
conflict. It is
well-opened
if it is strict
with exactly one initial move.

The function
\kappa_{A}
assigns a value to each configuration.
Configurations with payoff
0
are called
complete
: they
correspond to
terminated
executions, which have reached an
adequate stopping point where all calls have adequately
returned – we write
\mathscr{C}^{0}(A)
for the set of complete configurations
on
A
.
Otherwise,
\kappa_{A}
assigns a responsibility for non-completeness. If

\kappa_{A}(x)=-1
then Player is responsible, otherwise it is
Opponent.

The earlier constructions on arenas specialize into constructions on
boards. If
A
is a board, then the
dual

A^{\perp}
has payoff

\kappa_{A^{\perp}}=-\kappa_{A}
. If
A
and
B
are boards, then we set
the
tensor
(
resp.
the
par
) as having as
underlying arena the tensor, and payoff
\kappa_{A\otimes B}(x_{A}\otimes x_{B})=\kappa_{A}(x_{A})\otimes\kappa_{B}(x_{B})
(
resp.

\kappa_{A\invamp B}(x_{A}\otimes x_{B})=\kappa_{A}(x_{A})\invamp\kappa_{B}(x_{B})
), using the operations on payoff defined in

\begin{array}[]{c|S[table-format=1.0]S[table-format=1.0]S[table-format=1.0]}\hline\cr\hline\cr\otimes&\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{gray!25}{0}&\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}\\
\hline\cr\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}\\
\pagecolor{gray!25}{0}&\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{gray!25}{0}&\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}\\
\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}&\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}&\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{c|S[table-format=1.0]S[table-format=1.0]S[table-format=1.0]}\hline\cr\hline\cr{\invamp}&\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{gray!25}{0}&\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}\\
\hline\cr\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}\\
\pagecolor{gray!25}{0}&\pagecolor{red!25}{-1}&\pagecolor{gray!25}{0}&\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}\\
\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}&\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}&\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}&\pagecolor{blue!25}{1}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 2.
Payoff tables for operations on arenas, with
A\invamp B=(A^{\perp}\otimes B^{\perp})^{\perp}
.

Figure
2
. If
A,B
are
-
-boards, then the

hom-board
is defined as
A\vdash B=A^{\perp}\invamp B
,

i.e.
with
\kappa_{A\vdash B}=\kappa_{A^{\perp}}(x_{A})\invamp\kappa_{B}(x_{B})
.

Note that by definition of payoff, the order-isomorphism of
(
5
) refines to bijections:

\displaystyle-\otimes-\quad:\quad\mathscr{C}^{0}(A)\times\mathscr{C}^{0}(B)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}^{0}(A\otimes B)\,,

(8)

\displaystyle-\invamp-\quad:\quad\mathscr{C}^{0}(A)\times\mathscr{C}^{0}(B)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}^{0}(A\invamp B)\,.

(9)

Winning strategies.

We turn to strategies. The payoff is taken into account in the definition, as follows:

{defi}

Consider
A
a board, and
\sigma:A
a visible strategy.

We say that
\sigma
is
winning
if for all
x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\sigma)
, we have
\kappa_{A}(\partial_{\sigma}\,x^{\sigma})\geq 0
.

It is rather easy to show that copycat strategies are winning, and that
winning strategies are stable under composition
[
Cla24
, Proposition
8.2.15]
. Thus we obtain a bicategory
\mathbf{Wis}
with objects the

-
-boards, morphisms from
A
to
B
the winning strategies on
A\vdash B
, and
2
-cells the positive morphisms.

7.1.2.
Adjusting the pseudofunctor

We now show how to adjust
the pseudofunctor of Section
6.1.3
to account
for the new structure we have just introduced.

Firstly, as before, to each
-
-board
A
we shall associate a
groupoid. However, it shall not be the groupoid
\mathscr{S}(A)
of
symmetries between
all
configurations as before, instead we set

\|A\|
as the groupoid
\mathscr{G}(A)
having as objects the set
\mathscr{C}^{0}(A)
of
configurations of
null payoff
, with morphisms from
x_{A}
to

y_{A}
still comprising all the symmetries in
A
.

The same definition as in Section
5.2
now
yields a distributor

\|\sigma\|:\|A\|^{\mathrm{op}}\times\|B\|\to\mathbf{Set}

with those restricted groupoids – we keep the same definition for

\|\sigma\|
, which should cause no confusion as this notation shall
remain fixed until the end of the paper. As before, we have:

Theorem 29
.

We have
\|-\|:\mathbf{Wis}\to\mathbf{Dist}
a pseudofunctor.

Proof 7.1
.

The new definition of
\mathscr{G}(A)
imposes only one new proof obligation,
namely that for
\sigma:A\vdash B
,
\tau:B\vdash C
,
x_{A}\in\mathscr{G}(A)
and
x_{C}\in\mathscr{G}(B)
, then

\mathsf{pcomp}^{\sigma,\tau}_{x_{A},x_{C}}:\|\tau\odot\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{C})\to(\|\tau\|\bullet\|\sigma\|)(x_{A},x_{C})

as defined in the proof of Proposition
23
, is still
well-defined. Indeed, it sends a witness
(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\tau}\odot x^{\sigma},\theta_{C}^{+})\in\|\tau\odot\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{C})
to (the equivalence
class of) the pair

((\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}}),(\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}},x^{\tau},\theta_{C}^{+}))\in(\|\tau\|\bullet\|\sigma\|)(x_{A},x_{C})

for
x^{\sigma}_{B}=x^{\tau}_{B}=x_{B}
, exploiting that
x^{\sigma}
and

x^{\tau}
are matching; but this assumes that
(\theta_{A}^{-},x^{\sigma},\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}})\in\|\sigma\|(x_{A},x_{B})
and
(\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}},x^{\tau},\theta_{C}^{+})\in\|\tau\|(x_{B},x_{C})
, which only makes sense provided

x_{B}
has null payoff (and thus lies in
\|B\|
).
Thus seeking a contradiction, assume that
\kappa_{B}(x_{B})=1
. But then

\kappa_{B\invamp C}(\partial_{\tau}(x^{\tau}))=-1\invamp 0=-1
, which is
impossible since

x^{\tau}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\tau)
and
\tau
is winning. Symmetrically if

\kappa_{B}(x_{B})=-1
then this contradicts that
\sigma
is winning
since
x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\sigma)
. Hence,
\kappa_{B}(x_{B})=0
as
required.

The other components of the pseudofunctor remain unchanged.

But we are not just interested in
\mathbf{Wis}
and
\mathbf{Dist}
: we need
a pseudofunctor relating the Kleisli bicategories for the
corresponding exponential modalities in the two models.

7.2.
The exponential modality on
\mathbf{Wis}
.

First, we detail the construction of the exponential modality on

\mathbf{Wis}
(written
\oc
), along with its algebraic structure.

7.2.1.
The exponential modality for boards

The construction
of
\oc A
as a countable symmetric tensor of copies of
A

(§
5.5
) can be extended to
-
-boards. Note that any
configuration of
\oc A
has a representation as
\parallel_{i\in I}x_{i}

for
I\subseteq_{f}\mathbb{N}
, and this representation is unique if we
insist that every
x_{i}
is non-empty. Using that, we set (all
x_{i}

below are non-empty):

\begin{array}[]{rcrclcl}\kappa_{\oc A}&:&\mathscr{C}(\oc A)&\to&\{-1,0,+1\}\\
&&\parallel_{i\in I}x_{i}&\mapsto&\bigotimes_{i\in I}\kappa_{A}(x_{i})\end{array}

that is well-defined because
\otimes
is associative on
\{-1,0,+1\}
. If
A
is a
-
-board, then this results in a
-
-board; but

\oc A
is never strict, even if
A
is strict.

7.2.2.
Strict boards

To precisely capture the
relationship between
\oc
and
\mathbf{Sym}
we use
strict
boards
(Def.
6.1.1
), where
\emptyset
has payoff 1 and is
not considered complete. Indeed, the situation with the empty
configuration was at the heart of the issue in
§
5.5.2
.
We now state the following key property: for strict boards, the two
constructions
\mathbf{Sym}
and
\oc
are equivalent.

Proposition 30
.

Consider a strict board
A
.
There is an adjoint equivalence of categories:

L^{\oc}_{A}:\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))\simeq\mathscr{G}(\oc A):R^{\oc}_{A}.

Proof 7.2
.

We first show that we can identify the objects of
\mathscr{G}(\oc A)
with
families
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
of objects of
\mathscr{G}(A)
, where
I
is a
finite subset of natural numbers. We observed above that any
configuration
x\in\mathscr{C}(\oc A)
can be uniquely written as
x=\parallel_{i\in I}x_{i}
where
I\subseteq_{f}\mathbb{N}
, and
x_{i}\in\mathscr{C}(A)
is non-empty for all
i\in I
; yielding a family

(x_{i})_{i\in I}
. In addition, as
\kappa_{\oc A}(x)=0
, by
definition of the tensor on payoff values we must have
\kappa_{A}(x_{i})=0
for every
i\in I
,
i.e.

x_{i}\in\mathscr{G}(A)
.
This representation of
x\in\mathscr{G}(\oc A)
as a family
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
is clearly injective. Surjectivity boils down to the fact that each

x_{i}
is non-empty, which follows from
\kappa_{A}(x_{i})=0
since
A
is
strict.

Thus, from right to left,
R^{\oc}_{A}
sends
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
to the
sequence
x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{n}}
for
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\}
sorted
in increasing order. From left to right,
L^{\oc}_{A}
sends
x_{1}\dots x_{n}
to

(x_{i})_{i\in\{1\dots n\}}
.

Although this equivalence only holds for
strict
arenas, it shall
suffice for our purposes.

From now on, we use implicitly and without further mention the
representation of complete configurations of
\oc A
as families of
complete configurations of
A
.

7.2.3.
Relative pseudocomonads

The pseudofunctor
\mathbf{Wis}\to\mathbf{Dist}
does not preserve the
exponential modality as a pseudocomonad on
\mathbf{Wis}
, but as a
pseudocomonad
relative
to the sub-bicategory of
strict

arenas. We recall the categorical notions.

Recall that a monad on category
\mathcal{C}
relative to a
functor
J:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
is a functor
T:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
with a
restricted monadic structure, which we can use to form a Kleisli
category
\mathcal{C}_{T}
with objects those of
\mathcal{D}
. (Often,
\mathcal{D}
is a sub-bicategory of
\mathcal{C}
and
J
is the inclusion functor.)
This generalizes to
relative pseudomonads
[
FGHW18
]
and pseudocomonads:

{defi}

Consider
J:\mathcal{C}\to D
a pseudofunctor between bicategories.

A
relative pseudocomonad

T
over
J
consists of:

(1)
an object
TX\in\mathcal{D}
, for every
X\in\mathcal{C}
,

(2)
a family of functors
(-)^{*}_{X,Y}:\mathcal{D}[TX,JY]\to\mathcal{D}[TX,TY]
,

(3)
a family of morphisms
i_{X}\in\mathcal{D}[TX,JX]
,

(4)
a natural family of invertible
2
-cells,
for
f\in\mathcal{D}[TX,JY]
and
g\in\mathcal{D}[TY,JZ]
:

\mu_{f,g}:(g\circ f^{*})^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\Rightarrow}}g^{*}\circ f^{*}

(5)
a natural family of invertible
2
-cells, for
f\in\mathcal{D}[TX,JY]
:

\eta_{f}:f\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\Rightarrow}}i_{Y}\circ f^{*}

(6)
a family of invertible
2
-cells

\theta_{X}:i_{X}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\Rightarrow}}\mathrm{id}_{TX}
,
where
X,Y,Z
range over objects of
\mathcal{C}
, subject to the
coherence conditions in Figure
3
.

\textstyle{(h\circ(g\circ f^{*})^{*})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{g\circ f^{*},h}}
\scriptstyle{(h\circ\mu_{f,g})^{*}}
\textstyle{(h\circ(g^{*}\circ f^{*}))^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(h^{*}\circ(g\circ f^{*})^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{*}\circ\mu_{f,g}}
\textstyle{((h\circ g^{*})\circ f^{*})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{f,h\circ g^{*}}}
\textstyle{h^{*}\circ(g^{*}\circ f^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(h\circ g^{*})^{*}\circ f^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{h,g}\circ f^{*}}
\textstyle{(h^{*}\circ g^{*})\circ f^{*}}

\textstyle{f^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{f}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(i_{Y}\circ f^{*})^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{f,i_{Y}}}
\textstyle{i_{Y}^{*}\circ f^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{Y}\circ f^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{id}_{TY}\circ f^{*}}

Figure 3.
Coherence conditions for relative pseudocomonads

Those conditions are just what is needed to form a Kleisli
bicategory written
\mathcal{D}_{T}
, with
objects
those of
\mathcal{C}
,

morphisms
and
2
-cells
from
X
to
Y
the category

\mathcal{D}[TX,JY]
. We can compose
f\in\mathcal{D}[TX,JY]
and
g\in\mathcal{D}[TY,JZ]
as
g\circ_{T}f=g\circ f^{*}
, and
the
identity
on
X
is
i_{X}
.

7.2.4.
The exponential relative pseudocomonad

We now form a
concrete relative pseudocomonad on
\mathbf{Wis}
. Here,
\mathcal{C}
is
the sub-bicategory
\mathbf{Wis}_{s}
of
strict
arenas, and
J:\mathbf{Wis}_{s}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Wis}
the embedding. Note that even if
A
is strict,

\oc A
is not strict, and so
\oc
is not an endo(pseudo)functor;
instead we have
\oc:\mathbf{Wis}_{s}\to\mathbf{Wis}
.

We now outline the components in Definition
7.2.3
. For the component
(2)
, we must introduce additional
notions. Fix an
injection
\langle-,-\rangle:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
. If
I\subseteq_{f}\mathbb{N}
and
J_{i}\subseteq_{f}\mathbb{N}
for all
i\in I
, write
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}\subseteq_{f}\mathbb{N}
for the set of all
\langle i,j\rangle
for
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
. Then, we may define:

{defi}

Consider
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc A,B]
.

Its
promotion

\sigma^{\oc}
has ess
\oc\sigma
, and display
map the unique map of ess such that

\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma^{\oc}}((x^{\sigma,i})_{i\in I})=(x^{\sigma,i}_{A,j})_{\langle i,j\rangle\,\in\,\bigsqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}}\vdash(x^{\sigma,i}_{B})_{i\in I}

(10)

where
\partial_{\sigma}(x^{\sigma,i})=(x^{\sigma,i}_{A,j})_{j\in J_{i}}\vdash x^{\sigma,i}_{B}
for all
i\in I
.

For
(3)
, the
dereliction

\mathrm{der}_{A}\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc A,A]
on
strict
A
has ess
\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}
, and display map
\partial_{\mathrm{der}_{A}}(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x})=(x)_{\{0\}}\vdash x
. For
(4)
, we shall use a positive
isomorphism

\mathsf{join}_{\sigma,\tau}:(\tau\odot\sigma^{\oc})^{\oc}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\Rightarrow}}\tau^{\oc}\odot\sigma^{\oc}

sending
(x^{\tau}_{i}\odot(x^{\sigma}_{i,j})_{j\in J_{i}})_{i\in I}
to

(x^{\tau}_{i})_{i\in I}\odot(x^{\sigma}_{i,j})_{\langle i,j\rangle\in\bigsqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}}
. For
(5)
, given
B
strict and
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc A,B]
we have a positive iso
\mathsf{runit}_{\sigma}:\sigma\cong\mathrm{der}_{B}\odot\sigma^{\oc}
sending
x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\sigma)
to

\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x^{\sigma}_{B}}\odot(x^{\sigma})_{\{0\}}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\mathrm{der}_{B}\odot\sigma^{\oc})
. Finally, for
(6)
we have

\mathsf{lunit}_{A}:\mathrm{der}_{A}^{\oc}\cong\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{\oc A}
sending
(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x_{i}})_{i\in I}
to
\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{(x_{i})_{i\in I}}
. For all those, we use the fact that
positive isos are entirely determined by their action on
+
-covered
configurations, see
e.g.

[
Cla24
, Lemma 7.2.11]
.

Altogether, this gives us:

Theorem 31
.

The components described above define a pseudocomonad
\oc
relative to
the embedding of
\mathbf{Wis}_{s}
into
\mathbf{Wis}
.

The naturality and coherence laws follow from lengthy but direct
calculations, which we omit.
In particular, there is a Kleisli bicategory
\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}
whose
objects are strict boards – in the next section we shall see that this
is a cartesian closed bicategory.

7.3.
The exponential modality on
\mathbf{Dist}
.

For the sake of
relating
\mathbf{Wis}
and
\mathbf{Dist}
, we also need to properly introduce the
exponential modality structure of
\mathbf{Sym}(-)
on
\mathbf{Dist}
, which we shall
also present as a relative pseudocomonad to ease the correspondence.

7.3.1.
The groupoid
\mathbf{Sym}(A)
.

For
A
a groupoid, the objects of
\mathbf{Sym}(A)
are sequences of objects of

A
, written
\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\rangle
, or just
\langle a_{i}\rangle_{i\in n}
–
implicitly treating
n
as the set
\{1,\dots,n\}
.
There can be a morphism from
\langle a_{i}\rangle_{i\in n}
to
\langle b_{j}\rangle_{j\in p}
only when
n=p
, in which case it is a permutation

\pi
on
n
, along with a family comprising
f_{i}\in A(a_{\pi(i)},b_{i})
for each
i\in n
, written

\langle f_{i}\rangle^{\pi}_{i\in n}\in\mathbf{Sym}(A)(\langle a_{i}\rangle_{i\in n},\langle b_{j}\rangle_{j\in n})\,,

which we write simply as
\langle f_{i}\rangle_{i\in n}
when
\pi
is the
identity. We insist that we have
f_{i}\in A(a_{\pi(i)},b_{i})
rather
than
f_{i}\in A(a_{i},b_{\pi(i)})
: the family
(f_{i})_{i\in n}
is
thought of as indexed by the codomain, not the domain. This distinction
will make calculations easier later on.

We shall also write
\langle a_{i,j}\rangle_{i\in n,j\in p_{i}}\in\mathbf{Sym}(A)

for the concatenated sequence
\vec{a_{1,j}}\dots\vec{a_{n,j}}
.

7.3.2.
Promotion and dereliction in
