\mathbf{Dist}
.

Next, we present the
relative pseudocomonad structure on
\mathbf{Dist}
. We follow Definition

7.2.3
, even though this is not really
relative; it shall be a pseudocomonad “relative” to the inclusion of

\mathbf{Dist}
in
\mathbf{Dist}
. For this structure, we shall keep the same
notations as in Definition
7.2.3
, to avoid
notational collisions with the relative pseudocomonad in games.

We start with promotion. Recall that if
\alpha\in\mathbf{Dist}(\mathbf{Sym}(A),B)
,
the coend formula

\alpha^{\dagger}(\vec{a},\langle b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\rangle)=\int^{\vec{a_{1}},\dots,\vec{a_{n}}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\alpha(\vec{a_{i}},b_{i})\right)\times\mathbf{Sym}(A)[\vec{a_{1}}\dots\vec{a_{n}},\vec{a}]

yields a distributor
\alpha^{\dagger}\in\mathbf{Dist}[\mathbf{Sym}(A),\mathbf{Sym}(B)]
,
called the
promotion
of
\alpha
. Concretely, this means that
witnesses in
\alpha^{\dagger}(\vec{a},\langle b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\rangle)
consist
in the choice of three components

\displaystyle(\vec{a_{1}},\dots,\vec{a_{n}}\in\mathbf{Sym}(A),\quad\langle s_{i}\rangle_{i\in n}\in\prod_{i=1}^{n}\alpha(\vec{a_{i}},b_{i})\,,\quad f\in\mathbf{Sym}(A)[\vec{a_{1}}\dots\vec{a_{n}},\vec{a}])\,,

(11)

subject to the following equivalence relation, for each
(f_{i}\in\mathbf{Sym}(A)[\vec{a_{i}},\vec{a^{\prime}_{i}}])_{1\leq i\leq n}
:

\displaystyle(\vec{a_{1}},\dots,\vec{a_{n}}\,,~\langle s_{i}\rangle_{i\in n}\,,~f\circ(f_{1}\dots f_{n}))

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle(\vec{a^{\prime}_{1}},\dots,\vec{a^{\prime}_{n}}\,,~\langle\alpha(f_{i},b_{i})(s_{i})\rangle_{i\in n}\,,~f)

(12)

for
s_{i}\in\alpha(\vec{a_{i}},b_{i})
,
f\in\mathbf{Sym}(A)[\vec{a^{\prime}_{1}}\dots\vec{a^{\prime}_{n}},\vec{a}]
.

Here, we make some notational simplifications.
Firstly, the data of the
\vec{a_{i}}
s is redundant, provided other
components are typed. Secondly, writing
\vec{a_{i}}=\langle a_{i,1},\dots,a_{i,p_{i}}\rangle
and
\vec{a}=\langle a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{p}\rangle
, then a
morphism
f\in\mathbf{Sym}(A)[\vec{a_{1}}\dots\vec{a_{n}},\vec{a}]
is some

\langle f_{i}\rangle^{\pi}_{i\in p}
for
\pi:p\simeq\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}
; but up
to (
12
) we can – and we will – always assume that
the
f_{i}
s are identities, and only
\pi
remains. So altogether, a
witness in
\alpha^{\dagger}(\vec{a},\langle b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\rangle)
as in
(
11
) is specified by

\llangle s_{i}\rrangle_{i\in n}^{\pi}\in\alpha^{\dagger}(\vec{a},\langle b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\rangle)

an expression
where
s_{i}\in\alpha(\vec{a_{i}},b_{i})
and
\pi:p\simeq\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}
; from now on we fix this notation
4
4

4

Note the double
brackets, which we adopt to ease the distinction with morphisms of

\mathbf{Sym}(A)
.
– note in passing that we may
omit the permutation for the identity,
e.g.

\llangle s_{i}\rrangle_{i\in n}=\llangle s_{i}\rrangle_{i\in n}^{\mathrm{id}}
.

For dereliction, we simply have
i_{A}(\langle a\rangle,a^{\prime})=A[a,a^{\prime}]
.

7.3.3.
Additional components.

We carry on with the additional
component of the relative pseudocomonad structure. For every
\alpha\in\mathbf{Dist}[\mathbf{Sym}A,B]
and
\beta\in\mathbf{Dist}[\mathbf{Sym}B,C]
, we need

\mu_{\alpha,\beta}:(\beta\bullet\alpha^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\cong\beta^{\dagger}\bullet\alpha^{\dagger}

a natural iso which, given
s_{i,j}\in\alpha(\vec{a_{i,j}},b_{i,j})
and

t_{i}\in\beta(\vec{b_{i}},c_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq p_{i}
,
\vec{a_{i,j}}=\langle a_{i,j,1},\dots,a_{i,j,k_{i,j}}\rangle
,

\pi_{i}:\sum_{j\in p_{i}}k_{ij}\simeq\sum_{j\in p_{i}}k_{ij}
and
\pi:\sum_{i,j}k_{ij}\simeq\sum_{i,j}k_{ij}
, is set to

(\mu_{\alpha,\beta})_{\vec{a},\vec{c}}(\llangle t_{i}\bullet\llangle s_{i,j}\rrangle_{j\in p_{i}}^{\pi_{i}}\rrangle_{i\in n}^{\pi})=\llangle t_{i}\rrangle_{i\in n}\bullet\llangle s_{i,j}\rrangle_{i\in n,j\in p_{i}}^{\pi\circ\sum\pi_{i}}\,.

For cancellation of dereliction we need, for any
\alpha\in\mathbf{Dist}[\mathbf{Sym}A,B]
, natural isos

\eta_{\alpha}:\alpha\cong i_{B}\bullet\alpha^{\dagger}\,,\qquad\qquad\theta_{A}:i_{A}^{\dagger}\cong\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{Sym}A}

set by
(\eta_{\alpha})_{\vec{a},b}(s)=\mathrm{id}_{b}\bullet\llangle s\rrangle
for
s\in\alpha(\vec{a},b)
; and

\theta_{A}(\llangle f_{i}\rrangle_{i\in n}^{\pi})=\langle f_{i}\rangle_{i\in n}^{\pi}
for
(f_{i}\in A[a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}])_{i\in n}
.

Theorem 32
.

This specifies a (relative) pseudocomonad
\mathbf{Sym}
on
\mathbf{Dist}
.

As usual, we refer to the Kleisli bicategory
\mathbf{Dist}_{\mathbf{Sym}}
as
\mathbf{Esp}
.

7.4.
Lifting
\|-\|
to the Kleisli bicategories

We show how to lift
\|-\|
to a pseudofunctor
\|-\|_{\oc}:\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}\to\mathbf{Esp}
.
(We give a direct proof, although
one could write down a notion of pseudofunctor between
relative pseudocomonads that lifts to the
Kleisli bicategories
[
Str72
]
.)

Before we delve into the proof, we introduce some additional notation.

7.4.1.
Additional conventions and notations

If
X
is a set,
write
\mathbf{Fam}(X)
for the set of families
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
indexed by
I\subseteq_{f}\mathbb{N}
. This also applies to categories: if
A
is a
category, then
\mathbf{Fam}(A)
has morphisms from
(x_{i})_{\in I}
to

(y_{j})_{j\in J}
given by a permutation
\pi:I\simeq J
, and a family

(f_{i}:x_{i}\in y_{\pi(i)})_{i\in I}
of morphisms in
A
– so that

\mathscr{G}(\oc A)\cong\mathbf{Fam}(\mathscr{G}(A))
.
If
I
is a finite subset of natural numbers, let
|I|
be its
cardinal and

\kappa_{I}:I\simeq|I|
be the unique monotone bijection.
If
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
is a family, for simplicity we write

(x_{i})_{|i|\in|I|}
for the reindexing

(x_{\kappa_{I}^{-1}(j)})_{j\in|I|}
. Similarly, if
I\subseteq_{f}\mathbb{N}
and
(J_{i})_{i\in I}
is a family with
J_{i}\subseteq_{f}\mathbb{N}
for all
i\in I
, we write

\kappa_{I,(J_{i})}:\bigsqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}\simeq\sum_{i\in I}|J_{i}|

for the bijection corresponding to arranging (encodings of) pairs

\langle i,j\rangle
in lexicographic order.

We also introduce a notation for morphisms in
\mathbf{Fam}(\mathcal{C})
,
in line with the earlier notation introduced for
\mathbf{Sym}(A)
.
(and

\mathbf{Sym}(C)
, viewed as a full subcategory): we shall sometimes write

(f_{j})_{j\in J}^{\pi}

for the element of
\mathbf{Fam}(\mathcal{C})[(x_{i})_{i\in I},(y_{j})_{j\in J}]
normally
consisting of

(\pi^{-1}:I\simeq J,(f_{\pi^{-1}(i)}\in\mathcal{C}[x_{i},y_{\pi(i)}])_{i\in I})\,,

notice that the family is indexed by its target index set instead of
the source, and that the permutation
\pi^{-1}
is given pointwise by
the action of
\pi
on all
j\in J
.

7.4.2.
Preservators for exponentials

To lift
\|-\|
to
the Kleisli bicategories, we must introduce explicit natural
isomorphisms acting on witnesses for dereliction and promotion. We
make repeated use of the actions of functors on distributors introduced
in Definition
3.4
.

Lemma 33
.

For any strict board
A
, there is a natural isomorphism

\mathsf{pder}_{A}:\|\mathrm{der}_{A}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}]\cong i_{\mathscr{G}(A)}\in\mathbf{Dist}[\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A)),\mathscr{G}(A)]

Proof 7.3
.

From the definition,
\|\mathrm{der}_{A}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}](\langle x_{i}\rangle_{i\in n},y)
is
non-empty iff
n=1
, in which case

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{der}_{A}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}](\langle x\rangle,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{der}_{A}\|((x)_{i\in\{1\}},y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{[(\theta^{-})^{\{0\}\simeq\{1\}}_{\{0\}},\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{z}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\mathrm{der}_{A}),\theta^{+}]\mid\theta^{-}:x\cong_{A}^{-}z,~\theta^{+}:z\cong_{A}^{+}y\}

where
\partial_{\mathrm{der}_{A}}(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{z})=(z)_{\{0\}}\vdash z
. This is sent by
\mathsf{pder}_{A}

to

\theta^{+}\circ\theta^{-}\in\mathrm{der}_{\mathscr{G}(A)}[\langle x\rangle,y]

which is a bijection by Lemma
15
. Additional
verifications are routine.

Likewise, there is another natural isomorphism for preservation of promotion:

Lemma 34
.

For any
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc A,B]
, there is a natural isomorphism

\mathsf{pprom}_{\sigma}:\|\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}]\cong[R^{\oc}_{B}](\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\,,

between distributors in
\mathbf{Dist}[\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A)),\mathscr{G}(\oc B)]
.
Furthermore, it is natural in
\sigma
.

Proof 7.4
.

Recall that
+
-covered configurations of
\sigma^{\oc}
correspond to
families
(x^{\sigma,i})_{i\in I}
; and

\partial((x^{\sigma,i})_{i\in I})\quad=\quad(x^{\sigma,i}_{A,j})_{\langle i,j\rangle\,\in\,\bigsqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}}\vdash(x^{\sigma,i}_{B})_{i\in I}

provided
\partial(x^{\sigma,i})=(x^{\sigma,i}_{A,j})_{j\in J_{i}}\vdash x^{\sigma,i}_{B}
for all
i\in I
.

Now, consider
\langle x_{A,k}\rangle_{k\in p}\in\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))
, and

(x_{B,l})_{l\in L}\in\mathscr{G}(\oc B)
. We have:

\displaystyle\|\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}](\langle x_{A,k}\rangle_{k\in p},(x_{B,l})_{l\in L})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\sigma^{\oc}\|((x_{A,k})_{k\in p},(x_{B,l})_{l\in L})

which, by definition, is composed of triples

[(\theta^{-}_{i,j})^{\pi}_{\langle i,j\rangle\,\in\,\bigsqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}},\qquad(x^{\sigma,i})_{i\in I}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\sigma^{\oc}),\qquad(\theta^{+}_{l})_{l\in L}^{\varpi}]\quad\in\quad\|\sigma^{\oc}\|((x_{A,k})_{k\in p},(x_{B,l})_{l\in L})

where
\pi:\bigsqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}\simeq p
is a bijection,

\theta^{-}_{i,j}:x_{A,\pi\langle i,j\rangle}\cong_{A}^{-}x^{\sigma,i}_{A,j}
,
and
(\theta^{+}_{l})_{L}^{\varpi}:(x^{\sigma,i}_{B})_{i\in I}\cong_{\oc B}^{+}(x_{B,l})_{l\in L}
. This can be simplified: the positivity of

(\theta^{+}_{l})_{L}^{\varpi}
entails
L=I
and
\varpi=\mathrm{id}_{I}
. We are
left with

[(\theta^{-}_{i,j})^{\pi}_{\langle i,j\rangle\,\in\,\bigsqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}},\qquad(x^{\sigma,i})_{i\in I}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\sigma^{\oc}),\qquad(\theta^{+}_{i})_{i\in I}]\quad\in\quad\|\sigma^{\oc}\|((x_{A,k})_{k\in p},(x_{B,i})_{i\in I})\,.

We may finally define
\mathsf{pprom}_{\sigma}
to send the above witness to:

\llangle[(\theta^{-}_{i,j})^{\kappa_{J_{i}}}_{j\in J_{i}},x^{\sigma,i},\theta^{+}_{i}]\rrangle^{\overline{\pi}}_{|i|\in|I|}\in(\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}(\langle A,k\rangle_{k\in p},\langle x_{B,i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|})

where
\overline{\pi}
is the unique bijection such that the
composition
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\kappa_{I,(J_{i})}}}{{\simeq}}\Sigma_{i\in I}|J_{i}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{\pi}}}{{\simeq}}p
is

\pi
.

It is routine, if lengthy, to verify that this is a bijection, along
with naturality in
\sigma
.

By analogy with the
unitors
and
associators
forming a
pseudofunctor, we refer to the natural isomorphisms above as the

preservators
.

7.4.3.
Coherence of the preservators.

We show a series of lemmas expressing coherence between the
preservators and the components of the relative pseudocomonads.

First, we have compatibility with cancellation of dereliction,
expressed in two lemmas:

Lemma 35
.

For any strict board
A
, the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\|\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{\oc A}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{G}(\oc A)}[L^{\oc}_{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\|\mathrm{der}_{A}^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[R^{\oc}_{A}]\,\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[R^{\oc}_{A}](\|\mathrm{der}_{A}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[R^{\oc}_{A}]\,i_{\mathscr{G}(A)}^{\dagger}}

with all arrows the obvious structural maps or obtained
by Lemmas
6
,

33
and
34
.

Proof 7.5
.

This diagram is an equation of natural isomorphisms between
distributors

\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{G}(\oc A)\to\mathbf{Set}\,,

so taking
\langle z_{i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|}\in\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))

and
(y_{i})_{i\in I}\in\mathscr{G}(\oc A)
, it boils down to an equality
between functions, checked pointwise. An element of
\|\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{\oc A}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}](\langle z_{i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|},(y_{i})_{i\in I})
comprises

\,[(\theta_{i}^{-})^{\pi}_{i\in I}\,,\qquad\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{(x_{i})_{i\in I}}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{\oc A})\,,\qquad(\theta^{+}_{i})_{i\in I}]\quad\in\quad\|\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{\oc A}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}](\langle z_{i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|},(y_{i})_{i\in I})\,.

where
\pi:I\simeq|I|
is any bijection,
\theta_{i}^{-}:z_{i}\cong_{A}^{-}x_{i}
and
\theta_{i}^{+}:x_{i}\cong_{A}^{+}y_{i}
.

Using the description of the action of
\mathsf{pder}
and
\mathsf{pprom}
on
witnesses outlined earlier, we calculate its image alongside the two
paths around the diagram. In both cases, we get

\langle\theta^{+}_{i}\circ\theta^{-}_{i}\rangle^{\overline{\pi}}_{|i|\in|I|}\in i_{\mathscr{G}(A)}^{\dagger}(\langle z_{i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|},\langle y_{i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|})=([R^{\oc}_{A}]\,i_{\mathscr{G}(A)}^{\dagger})(\langle z_{i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|},(y_{i})_{i\in I})\,,

where
\overline{\pi}:|I|\simeq|I|
is the only
bijection such that
\overline{\pi}\circ\kappa_{I}=\pi
.

This will show that the two models deal with Kleisli composition with
dereliction on the left in the same way. Similarly, though not quite
symmetrically, the next lemma deals with the Kleisli composition with
dereliction on the right hand side:

Lemma 36
.

Consider
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc A,B]
. Then the diagram commutes

\textstyle{\|\mathrm{der}_{B}\odot\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{\mathscr{G}(B)}\bullet(\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}}
\textstyle{(\|\mathrm{der}_{B}\|\bullet\|\sigma^{\oc}\|)[L^{\oc}_{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\|\mathrm{der}_{B}\|[L^{\oc}_{B}]\bullet(\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\|\mathrm{der}_{B}\|\bullet(\|\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\|\mathrm{der}_{B}\|\bullet[R^{\oc}_{B}](\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

with all arrows the obvious structural maps or obtained
by Lemmas
6
,
6
,

33
and
34
.

Proof 7.6
.

This diagram states an equality between natural isomorphisms
between distributors

\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{G}(B)\to\mathbf{Set}\,,

which again can be checked pointwise. Taking
\langle x_{A,i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|}\in\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))
and
x_{B}\in\mathscr{G}(B)
, an
element of
(\|\mathrm{der}_{B}\odot\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])(\langle x_{A,i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|},x_{B})
is formed of three components

\,[(\theta^{-}_{i})^{\pi}_{\langle 0,i\rangle\,\in\,\bigsqcup_{\{0\}}I}\,,\quad\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x^{\sigma}_{B}}\odot(x^{\sigma})_{\{0\}}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\mathrm{der}_{B}\odot\sigma^{\oc})\,,\quad\theta_{+}]

where
\pi:\bigsqcup_{\{0\}}I\simeq|I|
,
\theta^{-}_{i}:x_{A,i}\cong_{A}^{-}x^{\sigma}_{A,i}
, and
\theta^{+}:x^{\sigma}_{B}\cong_{B}^{+}x_{B}
.

Following the upper path in the diagram, this is sent to:

\displaystyle\mathrm{id}_{x_{B}}\bullet\llangle[(\theta^{-}_{i})^{\pi^{\prime}}_{i\in I},~x^{\sigma},~\theta^{+}]\rrangle

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle i_{\mathscr{G}(B)}\bullet(\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}(\langle x_{A,i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|},x_{B})

(13)

where
\pi^{\prime}:I\simeq|I|
is the unique bijection such that

\bigsqcup_{\{0\}}I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{snd}}}{{\simeq}}I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi^{\prime}}}{{\simeq}}|I|
is
\pi
.

On the other hand, following the lower path, we get:

\displaystyle\theta^{+}\bullet\llangle[(\theta^{-}_{i})^{\kappa_{I}}_{i\in I},~x^{\sigma},~\mathrm{id}_{x^{\sigma}_{B}}]\rrangle^{\overline{\pi}}\in\mathrm{der}_{\mathscr{G}(B)}\bullet(\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}(\langle x_{A,i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|},x_{B})\,,

(14)

for
\overline{\pi}:|I|\simeq|I|
the only
bijection such that
\bigsqcup_{\{0\}}I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\kappa_{\{0\},(I)}}}{{\simeq}}|I|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{\pi}}}{{\simeq}}|I|

is
\pi
. But recall that
\kappa_{\{0\},(I)}:\sqcup_{\{0\}}I\simeq|I|
is the bijection arranging pairs in lexicographic order,
and
\kappa_{I}:I\simeq|I|
is the unique monotone bijection.
From that and the definition of
\overline{\pi}
and
\pi^{\prime}
, it follows
that
\overline{\pi}\circ\kappa_{I}=\pi^{\prime}
, which entails that
(
14
) is equivalent to (
13
).

Finally, we have compatibility with the multiplication
\mathsf{join}/\mu
, expressed via:

Lemma 37
.

If
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc A,B]
and
\tau\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc B,C]
, then the
following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\|\tau^{\oc}\odot\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\|(\tau\odot\sigma^{\oc})^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\|\tau^{\oc}\|\bullet\|\sigma^{\oc}\|)[L^{\oc}_{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[R^{\oc}_{C}](\|\tau\odot\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\|\tau^{\oc}\|\bullet(\|\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[R^{\oc}_{C}]((\|\tau\|\bullet\|\sigma^{\oc}\|)[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\|\tau^{\oc}\|\bullet[R^{\oc}_{B}](\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[R^{\oc}_{C}](\|\tau\|\bullet\|\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\|\tau^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{B}]\bullet(\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[R^{\oc}_{C}](\|\tau\|\bullet[R^{\oc}_{B}](\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[R^{\oc}_{C}](\|\tau\|[L^{\oc}_{B}])^{\dagger}\bullet(\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[R^{\oc}_{C}](\|\tau\|[L^{\oc}_{B}]\bullet(\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[R^{\oc}_{C}]((\|\tau\|[L^{\oc}_{B}])^{\dagger}\bullet(\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger})}

with all arrows the obvious structural maps or obtained
by Lemmas
6
,
6
,

33
and
34
.

Proof 7.7
.

This diagram states an equality between natural isomorphisms
between distributors

\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{G}(\oc C)\to\mathbf{Set}

which again can be checked pointwise. Taking
\langle x_{A,l}\rangle_{l\in n}\in\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))
and
(x_{C,i})_{i\in I}\in\mathscr{G}(\oc C)
, an element of
\|\tau^{\oc}\odot\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}](\langle x_{A,l}\rangle_{l\in n},(x_{C,i})_{i\in I})

comprises

\,[(\theta^{-}_{i,j,k})_{\langle\langle i,j\rangle,k\rangle}^{\pi}\,,\quad(x^{\tau,i})_{i\in I}\odot(x^{\sigma,i,j})_{\langle i,j\rangle\,\in\,\bigsqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}}\,,\quad(\theta^{+}_{i})_{i\in I}]

where
\partial_{\tau}\,x^{\tau,i}=(x^{\tau,i}_{B,j})_{j\in J_{i}}\vdash x^{\tau,i}_{C}
,
\partial_{\sigma}\,x^{\sigma,i,j}=(x^{\sigma,i,j}_{A,k})_{k\in K_{i,j}}
, so that

\partial_{\sigma^{\oc}}((x^{\sigma,i,j})_{\langle i,j\rangle})=(x^{\sigma,i,j}_{A,k})_{\langle\langle i,j\rangle,k\rangle}\vdash(x^{\sigma,i,j}_{B})_{\langle i,j\rangle}\,,\qquad\partial_{\tau^{\oc}}((x^{\tau,i})_{i\in I})=(x^{\tau,i}_{B,j})_{\langle i,j\rangle}\vdash(x^{\tau,i}_{C})_{i\in I}

with
x^{\sigma,i,j}_{B}=x^{\tau,i}_{B,j}
,
\pi:\bigsqcup_{\langle i,j\rangle}K_{i,j}\simeq n
,
\theta^{-}_{i,j,k}:x_{A,\pi\langle\langle i,j\rangle,k\rangle}\cong_{A}^{-}x^{\sigma,i,j}_{A,k}
and
\theta^{+}_{i}:x^{\tau,i}_{C}\cong_{C}^{+}x_{C,i}
.

For the left hand side path, a careful calculation shows that this is sent to

\llangle[(x^{\tau,i}_{B,j})_{j\in J_{i}}^{\kappa_{J_{i}}},x^{\tau,i},\theta^{+}_{i}]\rrangle_{|i|\in|I|}\bullet\llangle[(\theta^{-}_{ijk})_{k\in K_{ij}}^{\kappa_{K_{ij}}},x^{\sigma,i,j},x^{\sigma,i,j}_{B}]\rrangle_{|i|\in|I|,|j|\in|J_{i}|}^{\pi^{\prime}}

where
\pi^{\prime}:n\simeq n
is the only bijection such that
\pi^{\prime}\circ\kappa_{I,(J),(K)}=\pi
, where
\kappa_{I,(J),(K)}:\bigsqcup_{\langle i,j\rangle}K_{i,j}\simeq\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{j\in J_{i}}|K_{ij}|=n
arranges the triples
(i,j,k)
in lexicographic
order.

Likewise, for the right hand side path, it is sent to

\llangle[(x^{\tau,i}_{B,j})_{j\in J_{i}}^{\kappa_{J_{i}}},x^{\tau,i},\theta^{+}_{i}]\rrangle_{|i|\in|I|}^{\omega}\bullet\llangle[(\theta^{-}_{ijk})_{k\in K_{ij}}^{\kappa_{K_{ij}}},x^{\sigma,i,j},x^{\sigma,i,j}_{B}]\rrangle^{\omega^{\prime}}_{|\langle i,j\rangle|\in|\sqcup_{i\in I}J_{i}|}

where
\omega:\sum_{i\in I}|J_{i}|\simeq\sum_{i\in I}|J_{i}|
is obtained as
\kappa_{\sqcup_{i}J_{i}}\circ\kappa_{I,(J)}^{-1}
– that is, it links the lexicographic ordering of pairs

(i,j)
, and their ordering following their representations

\langle i,j\rangle\in\mathbb{N}
. Likewise,
\omega^{\prime}:n\simeq n
is the
unique bijection such that
\omega^{\prime}\circ\kappa_{\sqcup_{i}J_{i},(K)}=\pi
– that is, it is
\pi
where
i,j,k
are ordered in the
lexicographic order of the pairs
\langle i,j\rangle,k
.

It is then routine to
show that these two witnesses are equivalent, as required.

7.4.4.
Preservation of Kleisli composition

Finally, we are
equipped to show how
\|-\|
lifts to the Kleisli bicategories.
Recall that, for
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc A,B]
, we have

\|\sigma\|_{\oc}=\|\sigma\|[R^{\oc}_{A}]\in\mathbf{Dist}[\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A)),\mathscr{G}(B)]\,.

Thus, we may set:

\mathsf{pid}^{\oc}_{A}=\mathsf{pder}_{A}:\|\mathrm{der}_{A}\|_{\oc}\cong i_{\mathscr{G}(A)}

for the natural isomorphism witnessing preservation of identity.
Likewise, for
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc A,B]
and
\tau\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc B,C]
, we set the natural iso witnessing preservation of composition as

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\mathsf{pcomp}^{\oc}_{\sigma,\tau}&:&\|\tau\odot\sigma^{\oc}\|_{\oc}&=&\|\tau\odot\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}]\\
&&&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{pcomp}_{\sigma,\tau}}}{{\cong}}&(\|\tau\|\bullet\|\sigma^{\oc}\|)[L^{\oc}_{A}]\\
&&&\cong&\|\tau\|\bullet\|\sigma^{\oc}\|[L^{\oc}_{A}]\\
&&&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{pprom}_{\sigma}}}{{\cong}}&\|\tau\|\bullet[R^{\oc}_{B}](\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\\
&&&\cong&\|\tau\|[L^{\oc}_{B}]\bullet(\|\sigma\|[L^{\oc}_{A}])^{\dagger}\\
&&&=&\|\tau\|_{\oc}\bullet\|\sigma\|_{\oc}^{\dagger}\end{array}

These definitions provide the necessary components for:

Theorem 38
.

This provides the data for a pseudofunctor

\|-\|_{\oc}:\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}\to\mathbf{Esp}\,.

Proof 7.8
.

Naturality of
\mathsf{pcomp}^{\oc}_{\sigma,\tau}
in
\sigma
and
\tau
is
direct by composition of natural isos. The coherence
diagrams follow by diagram chasing, relying on Lemmas

35
,
36
and
37
.

7.5.
Cartesian closed structure

We show the bicategory
\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}
is cartesian closed, using typical
constructions in concurrent games, in sufficient detail to keep the
paper self-contained.
For a precise definition of the structure of cartesian closed bicategories we refer to
[
Sav20
]
.

7.5.1.
Cartesian products in
\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}

The empty board
\top
, with
\kappa_{\top}(\emptyset)=1
, is strict,
and it is direct that
\top
is a terminal object in
\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}
,
since any negative strategy
A\vdash\top
must be empty.
Now if
A,B
are strict, the
product board

A\with B
is defined as
A\otimes B
, except that all events of
A

are in conflict with events of
B
. This means that
configurations of
A\with B
are either empty, or of the form
\{1\}\times x

for
x\in\mathscr{C}(A)
(written
\mathsf{i}_{1}(x)
) or
\{2\}\times x
for
x\in\mathscr{C}(B)
(written
\mathsf{i}_{2}(x)
). For the payoff, we set

\kappa_{A\with B}(\emptyset)=1
and
\kappa_{A_{1}\with A_{2}}(\mathsf{i}_{i}(x))=\kappa_{A_{i}}(x)
, making
A\with B
a strict arena.
By strictness, we have an isomorphism

L^{\with}_{A,B}:\mathscr{G}(A\with B)\cong\mathscr{G}(A)+\mathscr{G}(B):R^{\with}_{A,B}

which reflects the definition of binary products in
\mathbf{Esp}
.
The
first projection

\pi_{A}\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc(A\with B),A]

has ess
\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{A}
, with display map the map of ess characterized by

\partial(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x})=(\mathsf{i}_{1}(x))_{\{0\}}\vdash x\,,

with the
second projection
defined symmetrically.
If
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc\Gamma,A]
and
\tau\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc\Gamma,B]
,
their
pairing
has ess
\sigma\with\tau
and display map the
unique such that

\partial(\mathsf{i}_{1}(x^{\sigma}))=x^{\sigma}_{\oc\Gamma}\vdash\mathsf{i}_{1}(x^{\sigma}_{A})\in\mathscr{C}(\oc\Gamma\vdash A\with B)\,,

and likewise for
\partial(\mathsf{i}_{2}(x^{\tau}))
, yielding
\langle\sigma,\tau\rangle\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc\Gamma,A\with B]
. This extends to a functor

\langle-,-\rangle:\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A]\times\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,B]\to\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A\with B]
in a straightforward way.

Proposition 39
.

For any strict arenas
\Gamma,A
and
B
, there is an adjoint
equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A\with B]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quad\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A]\times\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,B]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{A}\odot(-)^{\oc},\pi_{B}\odot(-)^{\oc})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle-,-\rangle}

providing the data to turn
A\with B
into a cartesian product in the
bicategorical sense.

Proof 7.9
.

To witness the equivalence, we need three natural isomorphisms

\eta_{\sigma}:\sigma\cong\langle\pi_{1}\odot\sigma^{\oc},\pi_{2}\odot\sigma^{\oc}\rangle\,,\qquad\epsilon^{i}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}:\pi_{i}\odot\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle^{\oc}\cong\sigma_{i}

for
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A_{1}\with A_{2}]
,
\sigma_{i}\in\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A_{i}]
and
i\in\{1,2\}
.

We focus first on the former. Notice that for all
x^{\sigma}\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\sigma)
, if
x^{\sigma}
is non-empty then as
\sigma
is
negative and the
A_{i}
are strict, the minimal event of
x^{\sigma}
must
occur in
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
. We set

\begin{array}[]{rcrclcl}\eta_{\sigma}&:&\sigma&\cong&\langle\pi_{1}\odot\sigma^{\oc},\pi_{2}\odot\sigma^{\oc}\rangle\\
&&\emptyset&\mapsto&\emptyset\\
&&x^{\sigma}&\mapsto&\mathsf{i}_{i}(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x^{\sigma}_{A_{i}}}\odot(x^{\sigma})_{\{0\}})&&\text{(if $x^{\sigma}\neq\emptyset$, $\partial(x^{\sigma})=x^{\sigma}_{\Gamma}\vdash\mathsf{i}_{i}(x^{\sigma}_{A_{i}})$)}\,,\end{array}

yielding a positive isomorphism, additionally natural in
\sigma
. For
the co-unit, we set

\begin{array}[]{rcrclcl}\epsilon^{i}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}&:&\pi_{i}\odot\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle^{\oc}&\cong&\sigma_{i}\\
&&\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x^{\sigma_{i}}_{A_{i}}}\odot(\mathsf{i}_{i}(x^{\sigma_{i}}))_{\{0\}}&\mapsto&x^{\sigma_{i}}\end{array}

again a positive isomorphism natural in
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
. For
the adjunction, we check that

\textstyle{\pi_{i}\odot\sigma^{\oc}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip-40.0pt\pi_{i}\odot\eta_{\sigma}^{\oc}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\pi_{i}\odot\langle\pi_{1}\odot\sigma^{\oc},\pi_{2}\odot\sigma^{\oc}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{i}_{\pi_{1}\odot\sigma^{\oc},\pi_{2}\odot\sigma^{\oc}}}
\textstyle{\pi_{i}\odot\sigma^{\oc}}

\textstyle{\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip-50.0pt\eta_{\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\langle\pi_{1}\odot\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle^{\oc},\pi_{2}\odot\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle^{\oc}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle\epsilon^{1}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}},\epsilon^{2}_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\rangle}
\textstyle{\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle}

commute for all
i=1,2
,
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A_{1}\with A_{2}]
,
\sigma_{i}\in\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A_{i}]
, which is direct.

7.5.2.
Closed structure in
\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}

Recall that the objects of
\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}
are strict boards, and observe
that any strict board
B
is isomorphic to
\with_{i\in I}B_{i}
where
the
B_{i}
are
pointed
, meaning that they have exactly one
minimal event. We first define a
linear arrow
for a
-
-board

A
and a pointed strict board
B
, by setting
A\multimap B
to be
A\vdash B
with a stricter dependency order, so that all events in
A

causally depend on the unique minimal move in
B
.

This is generalized for any strict
B
as

A\multimap\with_{i\in I}B_{i}=\with_{i\in I}(A\multimap B_{i})\,

whose configurations have a convenient description:

Lemma 40
.

For any
-
-board
A
, and strict board
B
, we have

(-\multimap-):\mathscr{C}^{0}(A)\times\mathscr{C}^{0}(B)\cong\mathscr{C}^{0}(A\multimap B)\,.

Proof 7.10
.

Straightforward, using crucially the fact that since
B
is strict, a
configuration of null payoff in
B
must be non-empty, so that the
A

component is always reachable.

Now for
A,B
strict, we define the
arrow

A\Rightarrow B
as

\oc A\multimap B
. This is equipped with

\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc((\oc A\multimap B)\with A),B]

an
evaluation
strategy
consisting of the ess
\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{\oc A\multimap B}
, and where the display map
is

\partial(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{(x^{i}_{A})_{i\in I}\multimap x_{B}})=(\mathsf{i}_{1}((x^{i}_{A})_{i\in I}\multimap x_{B}))_{\{\langle 0,0\rangle\}}\uplus(\mathsf{i}_{2}(x^{i}_{A}))_{\langle 1,i\rangle\in\Sigma_{\{1\}}I}\vdash x_{B}

if
x_{B}\neq\emptyset
, and empty otherwise,
with
\uplus
the union of families with disjoint index sets.
Likewise, the
currying
of
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}[\oc(\Gamma\with A),B]
is a strategy
\Lambda(\sigma)
with ess
\sigma
and display

\partial_{\Lambda(\sigma)}(x^{\sigma})=(x_{\Gamma}^{i})_{i\in I}\vdash(x_{A}^{j})_{j\in J}\multimap x_{B}

for
x^{\sigma}\neq\emptyset
, where

\partial_{\sigma}(x^{\sigma})=(\mathsf{i}_{1}(x_{\Gamma}^{i}))_{i\in I}\uplus(\mathsf{i}_{2}(x_{A}^{j}))_{j\in J}\vdash x_{B}
.

Altogether, this gives a functor
\Lambda:\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma\with A,B]\to\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A\Rightarrow B]
.

Proposition 41
.

For any strict
\Gamma,A
and
B
, there is an adjoint equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A\Rightarrow B]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma\with A,B]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}\odot\langle-\odot\pi_{\Gamma}^{\oc},\pi_{A}\rangle^{\oc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(-)}

providing the data to turn
A\Rightarrow B
into an exponential object in the bicategorical sense.

Proof 7.11
.

To witness the equivalence, we need two natural isomorphisms

\eta_{\sigma}:\sigma\cong\Lambda(\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}\odot\langle\sigma\odot\pi_{\Gamma}^{\oc},\pi_{A}\rangle^{\oc})\,,\qquad\beta_{\tau}:\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}\odot\langle\Lambda(\tau)\odot\pi_{\Gamma}^{\oc},\pi_{A}\rangle^{\oc}\cong\tau

for
\sigma\in\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma,A\Rightarrow B]
and
\tau\in\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}[\Gamma\with A,B]
. For the former, we set

\eta_{\sigma}(x^{\sigma})=\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{(x_{A,i})_{i}\multimap x_{B}}\odot((\mathsf{i}_{1}(x^{\sigma}\odot(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x_{\Gamma,j}})_{j\in J}))_{\langle 0,0\rangle}\uplus(\mathsf{i}_{2}(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x_{A,i}}))_{\langle 1,i\rangle})

where
\partial_{\sigma}(x^{\sigma})=(x_{\Gamma,j})_{j\in J}\vdash(x_{A,i})_{i\in I}\multimap x_{B}
. A careful check shows this is
well-defined and

\partial_{\Lambda(\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}\odot\langle\sigma\odot\pi_{\Gamma}^{\oc},\pi_{A}\rangle^{\oc})}(\eta_{\sigma}(x^{\sigma}))=(x_{\Gamma,j})_{\langle\langle 0,0\rangle,\langle j,0\rangle\rangle}\vdash(x_{A,i})_{\langle\langle 1,i\rangle,0\rangle}\multimap x_{B}

which is clearly positively symmetric to
\partial_{\sigma}(x^{\sigma})
as
required. Likewise, the expression

y=\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{(x_{A,i})_{i}\multimap x_{B}}\odot((\mathsf{i}_{1}(x^{\tau}\odot(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x_{\Gamma,j}})_{j\in J}))_{\langle 0,0\rangle}\uplus(\mathsf{i}_{2}(\mathbf{\hskip 2.22221ptc\!\!\!\!c\,}_{x_{A,i}}))_{\langle 1,i\rangle}

captures exactly all
+
-covered configurations of
\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}\odot\langle\Lambda(\tau)\odot\pi_{\Gamma}^{\oc},\pi_{A}\rangle^{\oc}
, displaying
to

(\mathsf{i}_{1}(x_{\Gamma,j}))_{\langle\langle 0,0\rangle,\langle j,0\rangle\rangle}\uplus(\mathsf{i}_{2}(x_{A,i}))_{\langle\langle 1,i\rangle,0\rangle}\vdash x_{B}

for
\partial_{\tau}(x^{\tau})=(\mathsf{i}_{1}(x_{\Gamma,j}))_{j\in J}\uplus(\mathsf{i}_{2}(x_{A,i}))_{i\in I}\vdash x_{B}
; we set
\beta_{\tau}(y)=x^{\tau}
. Unfolding these definitions, a careful calculation confirms
that the triangle identities hold.

7.5.3.
Cartesian closed structure in
\mathbf{Esp}
.

Here, we briefly
review the cartesian closed structure of
\mathbf{Esp}
. Firstly, we have

\top
the empty groupoid, with an adjoint equivalence

\mathbf{Esp}[\Gamma,\top]\simeq 1

for
1
the category with one object (and one morphism). For binary
products, given
A
and
B

groupoids, we simply set their
with
as the disjoint sum
A\with B=A+B
; writing as in games
\mathsf{i}_{1}(a)\in A+B
for
a\in A
and
\mathsf{i}_{2}(b)\in A+B
for
b\in B
.
For projections, we set distributors

\begin{array}[]{rcrcl}\pi_{1}&:&\mathbf{Sym}(A+B)^{\mathrm{op}}\times A&\to&\mathbf{Set}\\
&&(\langle\mathsf{i}_{1}(a)\rangle,a^{\prime})&\mapsto&A[a,a^{\prime}]\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{rcrcl}\pi_{2}&:&\mathbf{Sym}(A+B)^{\mathrm{op}}\times B&\to&\mathbf{Set}\\
&&(\langle\mathsf{i}_{2}(b)\rangle,b^{\prime})&\mapsto&B[b,b^{\prime}]\end{array}

and for the pairing of
\alpha\in\mathbf{Dist}[\mathbf{Sym}(\Gamma),A]
and
\beta\in\mathbf{Dist}[\mathbf{Sym}(\Gamma),B]
, we set

\begin{array}[]{rcrcl}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle&:&\mathbf{Sym}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{op}}\times(A+B)&\to&\mathbf{Set}\\
&&(\vec{\gamma},\mathsf{i}_{1}(a))&\mapsto&\alpha(\vec{\gamma},a)\\
&&(\vec{\gamma},\mathsf{i}_{2}(b))&\mapsto&\beta(\vec{\gamma},b)\end{array}

with obvious functorial action. We skip the details of the adjoint
equivalence

\mathbf{Esp}[\Gamma,A\with B]\simeq\mathbf{Esp}[\Gamma,A]\times\mathbf{Esp}[\Gamma,B]\,,

which are straightforward and not required for the remainder of this
paper.

Given groupoids
A
and
B
, the
arrow
is
A\Rightarrow B=\mathbf{Sym}(A)^{\mathrm{op}}\times B
. For
evaluation
,

\begin{array}[]{rcrcl}\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}^{\prime}&:&(\mathbf{Sym}(A\Rightarrow B)\times\mathbf{Sym}(A))^{\mathrm{op}}\times B&\to&\mathbf{Set}\\
&&((\langle(\vec{a},b)\rangle,\vec{a^{\prime}}),b^{\prime})&\mapsto&\mathbf{Sym}(A)[\vec{a^{\prime}},\vec{a}]\times B[b,b^{\prime}]\end{array}

and empty otherwise, provides the core of the evaluation mechanism; but
we need a distributor in
\mathbf{Esp}[\mathbf{Sym}(A)^{\mathrm{op}}\times B+\mathbf{Sym}(A),B]
.
For that, we set
\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}=\mathbf{ev}^{\prime}_{A,B}[L_{A\Rightarrow B,A}^{\mathsf{see}}]
,

i.e.

\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}(\vec{s},a)=\mathbf{ev}^{\prime}_{A,B}(L_{A\Rightarrow B,A}^{\mathsf{see}}(\vec{s}),b)

where
L_{A,B}^{\mathsf{see}}:\mathbf{Sym}(A+B)\simeq\mathbf{Sym}(A)\times\mathbf{Sym}(B):R_{A,B}^{\mathsf{see}}
is the Seely equivalence.

Finally, the
currying
of
\alpha\in\mathbf{Esp}[\Gamma+A,B]
is
defined by

\begin{array}[]{rcrcl}\Lambda(\alpha)&:&\mathbf{Sym}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\mathbf{Sym}(A)^{\mathrm{op}}\times B)&\to&\mathbf{Set}\\
&&(\vec{\gamma},(\vec{a},b))&\mapsto&\alpha(R^{\mathsf{see}}_{\Gamma,A}(\vec{\gamma},\vec{a}),b)\end{array}

informing an adjoint equivalence
\mathbf{Esp}[\Gamma,A\Rightarrow B]\simeq\mathbf{Esp}[\Gamma+A,B]
as in Proposition
41
.

7.6.
A cartesian closed pseudofunctor

We first show that the pseudofunctor
\|-\|_{\oc}:\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}\to\mathbf{Esp}

preserves the cartesian structure. The key observation is that:

Lemma 42
.

Consider
A,B
two mixed boards. Then, the distributor

\langle\|\pi_{A}\|_{\oc},\|\pi_{B}\|_{\oc}\rangle\in\mathbf{Esp}[\mathscr{G}(A\with B),\mathscr{G}(A)+\mathscr{G}(B)]

is naturally isomorphic to
\widehat{L^{\with}_{A,B}}
.

This is a straightforward variation on the proof that
\|-\|

preserves the identity. From this, we obtain that the pseudofunctor

\|-\|_{\oc}
preserves products:

Proposition 43
.

The pseudofunctor
\|-\|_{\oc}
is a
fp-pseudofunctor
in the
sense of
[
FS19
]
.

Proof 7.12
.

The terminal object is preserved in a strict sense, since
\mathscr{G}(\top)

is empty. For preservation of binary products, we must provide the
missing components for an adjoint equivalence

\textstyle{\mathscr{G}(A\with B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle\|\pi_{A}\|_{\oc},\|\pi_{B}\|_{\oc}\rangle}
\textstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathscr{G}(A)+\mathscr{G}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{q}^{\with}_{A,B}}

in
\mathbf{Esp}
. But the iso
L^{\with}_{A,B}:\mathscr{G}(A\with B)\cong\mathscr{G}(A)+\mathscr{G}(B):R^{\with}_{A,B}
lifts to an adjoint equivalence

\widehat{L^{\with}_{A,B}}:\mathscr{G}(A\with B)\simeq\mathscr{G}(A)+\mathscr{G}(B):\widehat{R^{\with}_{A,B}}

in
\mathbf{Esp}
; so providing
\mathsf{q}^{\with}_{A,B}=\widehat{R^{\with}_{A,B}}
, we conclude via Lemma
42
.

Finally, it remains to prove that the cartesian closed structure is
preserved as well. Observe that if
A
and
B
are
-
-boards with
B

strict, then we have an adjoint equivalence

L^{\Rightarrow}_{A,B}:\mathscr{G}(A\Rightarrow B)\simeq\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{G}(B):R^{\Rightarrow}_{A,B}

using first Lemma
40
as since
B
is strict, its
complete configurations are non-empty; and observing that this
decomposition also holds for symmetries; followed by Proposition

30
.

Now, as for binary products, our key observation is the following:

Lemma 44
.

Consider
A
and
B
two
-
-boards, with
B
strict. Then, the
distributor

\Lambda(\|\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}\|_{\oc}\bullet_{\oc}\mathsf{q}^{\with}_{A\Rightarrow B,A})\in\mathbf{Esp}[\mathscr{G}(A\Rightarrow B),\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{G}(B)]

is naturally isomorphic to
\widehat{L^{\Rightarrow}_{A,B}}
.

Proof 7.13
.

Unfolding the definitions on both sides, this boils down to a natural
isomorphism

\begin{array}[]{l}\|\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}\|((\mathsf{i}_{1}((x_{A,i})_{i\in I}\multimap x_{B}))_{0}\uplus(\mathsf{i}_{2}(y_{A,j}))_{j\in n},y_{B})\\
\qquad\qquad\cong\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))[\langle y_{A,j}\rangle_{j\in n},\langle x_{A,i}\rangle_{|i|\in|I|}]\times\mathscr{G}(B)[x_{B},y_{B}]\end{array}

which again is a variation on the preservation of the identity by

\|-\|
.

We may now use this to conclude:

Proposition 45
.

The pseudofunctor
\|-\|_{\oc}
is a
cc-pseudofunctor
in the
sense of
[
FS19
]
.

Proof 7.14
.

We must provide the missing components for an adjoint equivalence

\textstyle{\mathscr{G}(A\Rightarrow B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Lambda(\|\mathbf{ev}_{A,B}\|_{\oc}\bullet_{\oc}\mathsf{q}^{\with}_{A\Rightarrow B,A})}
\textstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathscr{G}(A)+\mathscr{G}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{q}^{\Rightarrow}_{A,B}}

in
\mathbf{Esp}
. But the equivalence
L^{\Rightarrow}_{A,B}:\mathscr{G}(A\Rightarrow B)\simeq\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{G}(B):R^{\Rightarrow}_{A,B}
lifts to

\widehat{L^{\Rightarrow}_{A,B}}:\mathscr{G}(A\Rightarrow B)\simeq\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(A))^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{G}(B):\widehat{R^{\Rightarrow}_{A,B}}

in
\mathbf{Esp}
; so providing
\mathsf{q}^{\Rightarrow}_{A,B}=\widehat{R^{\Rightarrow}_{A,B}}
,
we conclude via Lemma
44
.

Altogether this completes the proof of our main theorem:

Theorem 46
.

We have a cartesian closed pseudofunctor

\|-\|_{\oc}:\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}\to\mathbf{Esp}
.

Note.

We have constructed a cartesian closed
pseudofunctor directly, without leveraging the preservation of
symmetric monoidal structure established in earlier sections.
Although the cartesian closed structure is related to the monoidal
structure via Seely isomorphisms, the theory of bicategorical models
of linear logic, and a fortiori functors between models, is still
under-developed, and a direct approach seemed more achievable. This
is an important aspect of this work which deserves further investigation.

8.
Some consequences for the
\lambda
-calculus

Finally, for the last technical section of this paper, we illustrate
this pseudofunctor by relating a dynamic and a static model of the pure
(untyped)
\lambda
-calculus.

8.1.
Two models of the pure
\lambda
-calculus

8.1.1.
A reflexive object in
\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}

Our bicategory of games contains a
universal arena

\mathbf{U}
with
an isomorphism of arenas

\mathsf{unf}_{\mathbf{U}}:\ \ \mathbf{U}\ \ \cong\ \ \mathbf{U}\Rightarrow\mathbf{U}\ \ :\mathsf{fld}_{\mathbf{U}}

making
\mathbf{U}
an extensional reflexive object

[
Bar85
]
. Concretely,
\mathbf{U}
is constructed
corecursively as the solution of the following ‘domain-like equation’:

\mathbf{U}=\otimes_{\mathbb{N}}\oc\mathbf{U}\multimap o
, where
o
is the arena with
one negative move
*
,
\kappa_{o}(\emptyset)=1
and
\kappa_{o}(\{*\})=0
.

Following the interpretation of the
\lambda
-calculus in a
reflexive object, we have, for every closed
\lambda
-term
M
, a
strategy
\llbracket M\rrbracket:\mathbf{U}
. This strategy has a clear interpretation:
it is a representation of the
Nakajima tree
of
M
(see
e.g.
[
KNO02
,
CP18
]
).

8.1.2.
The Pure
\lambda
-Calculus in
\mathbf{Esp}

Likewise,
one can construct models of the
\lambda
-calculus in
\mathbf{Esp}

[
FGHW08
,
Oli21a
,
KMO23
]
. A
categorified graph model

[
KMO23
]
consists of a small category
\mathcal{D}
together with an embedding

\iota:\mathbf{Sym}(\mathcal{D})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{D}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}\,.

We adopt a type-theoretic understanding of these models, setting
\vec{a}\multimap a:=\iota(\vec{a},a).
The main intuition is that
elements of
\mathcal{D}
are
types
, where the arrow type is built exploiting
sequences of
types
. These sequences correspond to a notion of
k
-ary

intersection types

[
Oli21b
]
, by setting

(a_{1}\cap\cdots\cap a_{k}):=\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle
.
The
free
categorified graph model can be presented syntactically
as the category generated by the graph in Figure
4
.
Composition and identities are defined by induction in the natural way,
exploiting the definition of composition of morphisms of sequences
induced by
\mathbf{Sym}{(-)}
. We shall now
complete

\mathcal{D}
in an
appropriate way, producing an extensional model that we will then
relate to the universal arena
\mathbf{U}
.

Types:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\ni a::=\ast\mid\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle\multimap a

Proof-relevant subtyping:

id_{\ast}:\ast\to\ast

\vec{f}^{\sigma}:\vec{b}\to\vec{a}
f:a\to b
\vec{f}^{\sigma}\multimap f:(\vec{a}\multimap a)\to(\vec{b}\multimap b)

\sigma\in S_{k}
f_{1}:a_{\sigma 1}\to b_{1}
\cdots
f_{k}:a_{\sigma k}\to b_{k}
\langle f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\rangle^{\sigma}:\langle a_{1},\dots,a_{k}\rangle\to\langle b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\rangle

Figure 4.
Graph of intersection types
\mathsf{G}.

8.2.
Constructing an extensional model
\mathcal{D}^{\ast}

The extensional model
\mathcal{D}^{\ast}
is defined as an appropriate
completion of
D
, where we add chosen isomorphisms between types in
a coherent way. This construction can be defined in abstract
categorical terms as an appropriate pseudocolimit (a

coisoinserter
), but in the present paper we will just give its
explicit syntactic presentation. The following construction is a
particular case of
completion of partial algebras
for
categorified graph models, in the sense of
[
KMO23
]
.

We start by adding the following two arrows to the graph of intersection types
\mathsf{G}:

\mathsf{e}:\ast\to\langle\rangle\multimap\ast

\mathsf{e}^{-1}:\langle\rangle\multimap\ast\to\ast

resulting in a graph
\mathsf{G}^{\ast}
. We now want to obtain a category
from
\mathsf{G}^{\ast}
. Identities are the same as
\mathcal{D}
. Composition is inherited from the
composition of
\mathcal{D}
, by adding the following cases:

\mathsf{e}\circ\mathsf{id}_{\ast}=\mathsf{e}\qquad\mathsf{id}_{\langle\rangle\multimap\ast}\circ\mathsf{e}^{-1}=\mathsf{e}^{-1}\qquad\mathsf{e}\circ\mathsf{e}^{-1}=\mathsf{id}_{\langle\rangle\multimap\ast}\qquad\mathsf{e}^{-1}\circ\mathsf{e}=\mathsf{id}_{\ast}.

The category
\mathcal{D}^{\ast}
is a categorified graph model:

\iota^{\ast}:\mathbf{Sym}(\mathcal{D}^{\ast})^{op}\times\mathcal{D}^{\ast}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}^{\ast}\qquad(\vec{a},a)\mapsto\vec{a}\multimap a

Theorem 47

(
[
KMO23
]
)
.

The functor
\iota^{\ast}
is fully faithful and essentially surjective on objects.

Morphism actions and congruence on type derivations

We now explain how the interpretation of
\lambda
-terms in
\mathbf{Esp}
can be presented exploiting intersection types.

The intuition is that the semantics of a term will compute all its terminating executions in face of a given environement. These executions can be encoded by the means of an intersection type system.
Given
a\in\mathcal{D}
,

\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathbf{Esp}}^{\mathcal{D}}(a)
will appear (up to a canonical
isomorphism) as the set of derivations
of the judgement
\vdash M:a
in the type system. The construction of
this system reflects the corresponding operations on
distributors: in particular derivations carry explicit symmetries,

\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathbf{Esp}}^{\mathcal{D}}(a)
is really the set of derivations
quotiented
by an
equivalence relation letting symmetries flow though the concrete
derivation.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a categorified graph model. A
type declaration
over
\mathcal{D}
consists of a pair
x:\vec{a}
where
x
is a variable of the
\lambda
-calculus and
\vec{a}\in\mathbf{Sym}(\mathcal{D}).

A
type context
consists of a sequence of type declarations
x_{1}:\vec{a}_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\vec{a}_{n}.
Type contexts are denoted with greek letters
\gamma,\delta\dots.
Given two type contexts
\gamma=x_{1}:\vec{a}_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\vec{a}_{n}
and
\delta=x_{1}:\vec{b}_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\vec{b}_{n}
we define their
pointwise concatenation
as
\gamma\otimes\delta=x_{1}:\vec{a}_{1}::\vec{b}_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\vec{a}_{n}::\vec{b}_{n}
where
\vec{a}_{i}::\vec{b}_{i}
denotes list concatenation. A
morphism
of type contexts
\eta:\gamma\to\delta
consist of a family of morphisms
\vec{f}_{i}^{\sigma_{i}}:\vec{a}_{i}\to\vec{b}_{i}
for
i\in[n].
Morphisms of type contexts are denoted with greek letters
\eta,\theta\dots
The definition of the type system is given in Figure
5
.

Given a type derivation
\pi
of conclusion
\gamma\vdash M:a
and type morphisms
\theta:\delta\to\gamma
and
f:a\to b
we define contravariant and covariant actions on derivations:

\pi\{\theta\}

.

\delta\vdash M:a

[f]\pi

.

\gamma\vdash M:b

The explicit definitions are given, respectively, in Figures

6
and
8.2
. By a straighforward induction, one can
prove that both actions are compositional and unital in the appropriate
way, that is
(\pi\{\theta\})\{\theta^{\prime}\}=\pi\{\theta\circ\theta^{\prime}\},\pi\{id\}=\pi
and
[g]([f]\pi)=[g\circ f]\pi,[id]\pi=\pi
.

By the means of these morphism actions, we define an
equivalence
relation
on type derivations, as the smallest equivalence relation
generated by the rules of Figure
8
and that is compatible
with the structure of type derivations.
This equivalence corresponds to the one induced by an appropriate
coend formula
:
namely, the coend formula that derives from the categorified graph
model interpretations of application and
\lambda
-abstraction in
\mathbf{Esp}
.

Intersection type distributors

We are now ready to introduce the syntactic presentation of the categorified graph model semantics, namely
intersection type distributors
.

Given a
\lambda
-term
M
and a categorified graph model
\mathcal{D}
, with
\vec{x}\supseteq\mathsf{fv}(M)
, we define

\mathsf{ITD}^{\mathcal{D}}(M)_{\vec{x}}:(\mathbf{Sym}(\mathcal{D})\overbrace{\otimes\dots\otimes}^{\mid\vec{x}\mid\text{ times}}\mathbf{Sym}(\mathcal{D}))^{op}\times\mathcal{D}\to\mathbf{Set}

the
intersection type distributor
of
M
,
by the following family of sets:

\mathsf{ITD}^{\mathcal{D}}(M)_{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}}(\vec{a}_{1},\dots,\vec{a}_{n};a)=\left\{\raisebox{-10.0pt}{
\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 46.95477pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 1.50693pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hskip 2.85014pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\pi$}}\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox to10.33325pt{\leaders{\vbox to3.44444pt{\vss\hbox{.}\vss\par}}{\vfill}\par}\hbox to0.0pt{ \vbox{\vskip 5.16663pt\hbox{\ignorespaces}\vskip 5.16663pt\par}\hss}}}}\par}\par}}\hbox{$x_{1}:\vec{a}_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\vec{a}_{n}\vdash M:a$}}\par}\par}}
}\right\}/\sim\,.

Stating the connection between the intersection type distributor of a
term and its species of structure semantics requires the

Seely equivalence
for contexts:

L_{\vec{x}}^{\mathsf{see}}:\mathbf{Sym}(\mathcal{D}\overbrace{+\ldots+}^{\text{$|\vec{x}|$
times}}\mathcal{D})\simeq\mathbf{Sym}(\mathcal{D})\overbrace{\times\ldots\times}^{\text{$|\vec{x}|$ times}}\mathbf{Sym}(\mathcal{D}):R_{\vec{x}}^{\mathsf{see}}\,.

Theorem 48

(
[
KMO23
]
)
.

Let
M
be a
\lambda
-term,
\vec{x}\supseteq\mathsf{fv}(M)
and
\mathcal{D}
a categorified graph model.

Then, we have an isomorphism, natural in
\delta
and
a
:

\mathsf{ITD}(M)_{\vec{x}}(\delta,a)\quad\cong\quad\llbracket M\rrbracket_{\mathbf{Esp}}^{\mathcal{D}}(R_{\vec{x}}^{\mathsf{see}}(\delta),a)\,.

Relating the Interpretations

We first relate the reflexive

Objects:

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{U})\ni v::=(\bar{v}_{1},\dots,\bar{v}_{k})\multimap\ast\qquad(\bar{v}_{1}\ \text{non-empty.})

\displaystyle\bar{v}::=(v_{i})_{i\in I\subseteq_{f}\mathbb{N}}

Morphisms:

\theta_{i}:\bar{v}_{i}\cong\bar{v}^{\prime}_{i}
(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n})\multimap\ast:(\bar{v}_{1},\dots,\bar{v}_{n})\multimap\ast\to(\bar{v}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\bar{v}^{\prime}_{n})\multimap\ast

\pi:I\cong I^{\prime}
\theta_{i}:v_{i}\to v_{\pi i}
(\theta_{i})^{\pi}_{i\in I}:(v_{i})_{i\in I}\to(v^{\prime}_{j})_{j\in I^{\prime}}

Figure 9.
Groupoid
\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{U})
associated to the reflexive object
\mathbf{U}
.

Theorem 49
.

We have an adjoint equivalence of groupoids:

L^{\mathbf{U}}:\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{U})\simeq\mathcal{D}^{\ast}:R^{\mathbf{U}}
.

Proof 8.1
.

First,
\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{U})
is
isomorphic
to that
presented via the grammar in Figure
9
. Indeed,

\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{U})=\mathscr{G}(\otimes^{\mathbb{N}}\oc\mathbf{U}\multimap o)\cong\mathscr{G}(\otimes^{\mathbb{N}}\oc\mathbf{U})\times\mathscr{G}(o)

where
\mathscr{G}(o)=\{\emptyset\}
. Likewise, (the obvious infinitary
generalization of)
(
9
)
informs a bijection between

\mathscr{G}(\otimes^{\mathbb{N}}\oc\mathbf{U})
and the sequences
\prod_{n\in\omega}\mathscr{G}(\oc\mathbf{U})
which are empty almost everywhere – those may be
represented uniquely as sequence
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
where
x_{i}\in\mathscr{G}(\oc\mathbf{U})
and
x_{1}
is non-empty, removing the
heading infinite stream of empty configurations. Finally,
\mathscr{G}(\oc\mathbf{U})
is in bijection with families
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
for
I\subseteq_{f}\mathbb{N}
, as outlined in the proof of Proposition
30
. From
these observations, the claimed bijection is direct and extends to symmetries.

Hence, we treat Figure
9
as a syntactic presentation
of
\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{U})
. Armed with this, we may now define the components of
the desired equivalence simply by induction, with:

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}L^{\mathbf{U}}((\bar{v}_{1},\dots,\bar{v}_{k})\multimap\ast)&=&L^{\mathbf{U}}(\bar{v}_{1})\multimap\dots\multimap L^{\mathbf{U}}(\bar{v}_{k})\multimap\ast\\
L^{\mathbf{U}}((v_{i})_{i\in I})&=&\langle L^{\mathbf{U}}(v_{i_{1}}),\ldots,L^{\mathbf{U}}(v_{i_{n}})\rangle&&\text{(where $I=\{i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}\}$)}\\
\\
R^{\mathbf{U}}(\langle\rangle\multimap a)&=&R^{\mathbf{U}}(a)\\
R^{\mathbf{U}}(\vec{a}_{1}\multimap\ldots\multimap\vec{a}_{n}\multimap\ast)&=&(R^{\mathbf{U}}(\vec{a}_{1}),\ldots,R^{\mathbf{U}}(\vec{a}_{n}))\multimap\ast&&\text{($\vec{a}_{1}$ non-empty)}\\
R^{\mathbf{U}}(\langle a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\rangle)&=&(R^{\mathbf{U}}(a_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq n}\end{array}

which easily extends to an equivalence as claimed.

Additionally, this equivalence is compatible with unfoldings in the
sense that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{U}\Rightarrow\mathbf{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sym}(\mathcal{D}^{\ast})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sym}(\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{U}))^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{G}(\mathbf{U})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\mathbf{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{G}(\mathsf{unf}_{\mathbf{U}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{unf}_{\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\Rightarrow}_{\mathbf{U},\mathbf{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Sym}(L^{\mathbf{U}})^{\mathrm{op}}\times L^{\mathbf{U}}}

Figure 10.
Compatibility with unfoldings

the diagram of Figure
10
commutes, and compatible with
folding in the same way.

Using our Theorem
46
, it follows that:

Theorem 50
.

For any closed term
M
, we have a natural iso

\llbracket M\rrbracket^{\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}_{\mathbf{Esp}}\cong\|\llbracket M\rrbracket^{\mathbf{U}}_{\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}}\|_{\oc}\circ R^{U}

This shows that, for
a\in\mathcal{D}^{\ast}
, the set

\llbracket M\rrbracket^{\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}_{\mathbf{Esp}}(a)
described in Theorem
48
as a set of derivations up
to congruence may be equivalently described, up to canonical
isomophism, as

\{(x,\theta^{+})\quad\mid\quad x\in\mathscr{C}^{+}(\llbracket M\rrbracket^{\mathbf{U}}_{\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}}),\quad\theta^{+}:\partial(x)\cong_{\mathbf{U}}^{+}R^{\mathbf{U}}(a)\}\,.

In other words, the interpretation of
pure
\lambda
-terms as species computes the set of
+
-covered
configurations
equipped with
a positive
symmetry. Interestingly, this set is
constructed
without quotient
, and thus
provides canonical representatives for the equivalence classes of
derivations. We now include a detailed example
illustrating this by examining the interpretation of a simple

\lambda
-term in both models.

{exa}

Take the
\lambda
-term
M=xx.
We shall compute its species
interpretation on appropriate points and then compare it to its
concurrent game semantics.

The species interpretation of
xx
consists of a distributor

\llbracket xx\rrbracket^{\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}_{\mathbf{Esp}}(-;-):\mathbf{Sym}(\mathcal{D}^{\ast})^{op}\times\mathcal{D}^{\ast}\to\mathbf{Set}

We evaluate the functor on
\llbracket xx\rrbracket^{\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}_{\mathbf{Esp}}(\langle\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast,\ast,\ast\rangle;\ast)
. To compute an
element of this set, we shall exploit the type theoretic presentation
of the semantics. Following Figure
5
, an element of
\llbracket xx\rrbracket^{\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}_{\mathbf{Esp}}(\langle\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast,\ast,\ast\rangle;\ast)
can be defined by the following derivation
scheme:

f:c_{0}\to(\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle\multimap\ast)
x:\langle c_{0}\rangle\vdash x:\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle\multimap\ast

g_{1}:c_{1}\to b_{1}
x:\langle c_{1}\rangle\vdash x:b_{1}

g_{2}:c_{2}\to b_{2}
x:\langle c_{2}\rangle\vdash x:b_{2}

\eta
x:\langle\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast,\ast,\ast\rangle\vdash xx:\ast

written
\pi^{f,g_{1},g_{2}}_{\eta}
,
where
\eta=(f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}):(\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast,\ast,\ast)\to(c_{0},c_{1},c_{2})
. as a consequence of Theorem

47
, we have
c_{0}=\langle a_{1},a_{2}\rangle\multimap a

and
f=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle^{\tau}\multimap g
with
f_{1}:b_{\tau i}\to a_{i},g:a\to\ast.

Then, by the congruence on derivations
induced by the coend formula, we have that

\pi^{f,g_{1},g_{2}}_{\eta}\sim

id_{\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle\multimap\ast}
x:\langle\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle\multimap\ast\rangle\vdash x:\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle\multimap\ast

id_{b_{1}}
x:\langle b_{1}\rangle\vdash x:b_{1}

id_{b_{2}}
x:\langle b_{2}\rangle\vdash x:b_{2}

(f,g_{1},g_{2})\circ\eta
x:\langle\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast,\ast,\ast\rangle\vdash xx:\ast

Hence, we can restrict to the derivation scheme:

id_{\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle\multimap\ast}
x:\langle\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle\multimap\ast\rangle\vdash x:\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle\multimap\ast

id_{b_{1}}
x:\langle b_{1}\rangle\vdash x:b_{1}

id_{b_{2}}
x:\langle b_{2}\rangle\vdash x:b_{2}

\theta
x:\langle\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast,\ast,\ast\rangle\vdash xx:\ast

(15)

where
\theta=\langle h_{0},h_{1},h_{2}\rangle^{\sigma}
, where the permutation
\sigma
is either the identity or the swap between the two occurrences
of
\ast
. Actually, one can prove that there are just
two

different equivalence classes of derivations in
\llbracket xx\rrbracket^{\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}_{\mathbf{Esp}}(\langle\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast,\ast,\ast\rangle;\ast)
. These correspond precisely to the choice of
either the identity or the swap permutation in the following restricted
schema:

id_{\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast}
x:\langle\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast\rangle\vdash x:\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast

id_{\ast}
x:\langle\ast\rangle\vdash x:\ast

id_{\ast}
x:\langle\ast\rangle\vdash x:\ast

\theta
x:\langle\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast,\ast,\ast\rangle\vdash xx:\ast

(16)

where now
\theta=\langle h_{0},h_{1},h_{2}\rangle^{\sigma}
with
h_{i}
being the
appropriate identity morphism. This happens because for any isomorphim

b_{1}\cong\ast,b_{2}\cong\ast
, we can prove that
15
and
16
are equivalent by the rules of congruence
(Figure
8
).

Now, let us consider the game semantics side of things. As
x\,x
has
just one free variable,
\llbracket x\,x\rrbracket_{\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}}
is a strategy on
\oc\mathbf{U}\vdash\mathbf{U}
. For our example, the useful unfolding of that is
\oc(\otimes^{\mathbb{N}}\oc\mathbf{U}\multimap o)\vdash\mathbf{U}
– and following the equivalence
of Theorem
49
, the point
(\langle\langle\ast,\ast\rangle\multimap\ast,\ast,\ast\rangle;\ast)
corresponds to the configuration in the
diagram below:

\oc(\otimes^{\mathbb{N}}\oc\mathbf{U}
\multimap
o)
\vdash
\mathbf{U}
\mathsf{q}
\mathsf{q}_{0}
\mathsf{q}_{1}
\mathsf{q}_{2}
\mathsf{q}_{0}
\mathsf{q}_{1}

where the numbers in index are the copy indices, corresponding to the
distinct copies generated by the exponential modality –
x
is called
three times, and one of these calls its argument twice; the copy
indices simply follow the rank in the sequence representation in

\mathcal{D}^{\ast}
.
Now, the configurations of
\llbracket x\,x\rrbracket_{\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}}
whose display is
symmetric to the above turn out to be those of the form

\mathsf{q}
\mathsf{q}_{\langle 0,0\rangle}
\mathsf{q}_{i}
\mathsf{q}_{j}
\mathsf{q}_{\langle 1,\langle i,0\rangle\rangle}
\mathsf{q}_{\langle 1,\langle j,0\rangle\rangle}

for arbitrary
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
. If we insist that the display is

positively
symmetric to the configuration above, then this fixes

i=0
and
j=1
, so that there is only
one
such configuration of

\llbracket x\,x\rrbracket_{\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}}
. Any finally, there are exactly two positive
symmetries

\raisebox{-20.0pt}{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

as the only degree of freedom is whether or not to swap the two
standalone positive
\mathsf{q}
s. Hence, we recover the same number of
witnesses as in generalized species of structure; though as the reader
can observe, the reasoning and the objects of study are very different.

Finally, in what follows, we show how our cartesian closed
pseudofunctor allows us to transfer results from game semantics to
generalized species. It is known that the game semantics of the

\lambda
-calculus captures the maximal sensible
\lambda
-theory

\mathcal{H}^{*}
, because strategies coincide with the corresponding
normal forms, Nakajima trees – this was first shown in traditional
game semantics by Ker
et al

[
KNO02
]
,
and adapted to concurrent games (additionally with probabilistic
choice) – in
[
CP18
]
. Using Theorem

50
, we can deduce a result on the
\lambda
-theory induced
by
\mathcal{D}^{\ast}.
Given a model
\mathcal{D}
of
\lambda
-calculus in an
arbitrary bicategory, the
theory
induced by
\mathcal{D}
is the
\lambda
-theory induced by the relation on
\lambda
-terms:

\{(M,N)\mid M,N\in\Lambda\text{ s.t. }\llbracket M\rrbracket^{D}\cong\llbracket N\rrbracket^{D}.\}.

The correspondence established in this paper
allows us to derive the following new result:

Corollary 51
.

The theory of
\mathcal{D}^{\ast}
is
\mathcal{H}^{*}
.

Proof 8.2
.

Consider two
\lambda
-terms
M
and
N
.
If
M\equiv_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}N
, then
\llbracket M\rrbracket^{\mathbf{U}}_{\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}}\cong\llbracket N\rrbracket^{\mathbf{U}}_{\mathbf{Wis}_{\oc}}
and by Theorem
50
,

\llbracket M\rrbracket^{\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}_{\mathbf{Esp}}\cong\llbracket N\rrbracket^{\mathcal{D}^{\ast}}_{\mathbf{Esp}}
.

Reciprocally, isomorphisms in
\mathbf{Esp}
form a sensible
\lambda
-theory
(see
[
KMO23
]
, Corollary 6.14). As
H^{*}
is the greatest
sensible
\lambda
-theory, it must include isomorphisms in
\mathbf{Esp}
.

This is a simple application, but recent work suggests that there is much to explore in
the bicategorical semantics of the
\lambda
-calculus
[
KMO23
]
.

9.
Conclusion

In this paper, we have mapped out the links between thin concurrent games
and generalized species of structures, two bicategorical models of
linear logic and programming languages. By giving a proof-relevant
and bicategorical extension of the
relationship between dynamic and static models, we have established the new state of
the art in this line of work.

This bridges previously disconnected semantic realms. In the past,
such bridges have proved fruitful for transporting results between
dynamic and static semantics
(
[
CCPW18
,
CdV20
,
CP18
]
).
This opens up many perspectives: bicategorical models are a
very active field, and several recent developments may be re-examined in
light of this connection
(
[
Oli21a
,
TAO18
,
CdV20
]
).

Moreover, this work exposes fundamental phenomena
regarding symmetries in quantitative semantics. Symmetries lie at the heart of both thin
concurrent games and generalized species, but they are treated
completely differently: in
\mathbf{Esp}
, witnesses referring to multiple
copies of a resource are closed under the action of
all symmetries (“saturated”), whereas
\mathbf{TCG}
relies on a mechanism
for choreographing a choice of copy indices, providing an address for
individual resources (“thin”). This distinction is also at the
forefront of a recent line of work on
thin spans of groupoids

[
CF23
,
CF24
]
,
which shares with game semantics this handling of symmetry, without
the combinatorial details of event structures.
